Formula Size-Depth Tradeoffs for Iterated Sub-Permutation Matrix Multiplication
Benjamin Rossman

Abstract
We study the formula complexity of Iterated Sub-Permutation Matrix Multiplication , the logspace-complete problem of computing the product of k k n n -by- n n Boolean matrices with at most a single 1 1 in each row and column. For all d ≤ log ⁡ k d\leq\log k , this problem is solvable by n O ⁡ ( d ​ k 1 / d ) n^{O(dk^{\smash{1/d}})} size monotone formulas of two distinct types: (unbounded fan-in ) A ​ C 0 AC^{0} formulas of depth d + 1 d+1 and (semi-unbounded fan-in) S ​ A ​ C 0 SAC^{0} formulas of ⋀ \bigwedge -depth d d and ⋀ \bigwedge -fan-in k 1 / d k^{1/d} . The results of this paper give • matching n Ω ⁡ ( d ​ k 1 / d ) {n^{\Omega(dk^{\smash{1/d}})}} lower bounds for monotone 𝐴𝐶 0 \mathit{AC^{0}} and 𝑆𝐴𝐶 0 \mathit{SAC^{0}} formulas for all k ≤ log ⁡ log ⁡ n k\leq\log\log n , as well as • slightly weaker n Ω ⁡ ( d ​ k 1 / 2 ​ d ) \smash{n^{\Omega(dk^{\smash{1/2d}})}} lower bounds for non-monotone 𝐴𝐶 0 \smash{\mathit{AC^{0}}} and 𝑆𝐴𝐶 0 \smash{\mathit{SAC^{0}}} formulas. These size-depth tradeoffs converge at d = log ⁡ k d=\log k to tight n Ω ⁡ ( log ⁡ k ) n^{\Omega(\log k)} lower bounds for both unbounded-depth monotone formulas [ 21 ] and bounded-depth non-monotone formulas [ 23 ] . Our non-monotone lower bounds extend to the more restricted Iterated Permutation Matrix Multiplication problem, improving the previous n k 1 / exp ⁡ ( O ⁡ ( d ) ) n^{k^{\smash{1/\exp(O(d))}}} tradeoff for this problem [ 4 ] .



1
Introduction

Motivated by the question of
\mathit{NC^{1}}
vs.
\mathit{L}
(polynomial-size formulas vs. branching programs), we study the formula complexity of
Iterated Sub-Permutation Matrix Multiplication
(
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
),
the logspace-complete problem of computing the product of
k

n
-by-
n
sub-permutation matrices.
Our results give asymptotically tight size-depth tradeoffs for monotone (unbounded fan-in)
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and (semi-unbounded fan-in)
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas solving this problem, as well as slightly weaker tradeoffs for non-monotone formulas.

Our lower bounds are based on a method of
reducing
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}

to
simpler formulas called
join trees
, which construct the length-
k
path graph from its single-edge subgraphs via the union operation.
A large part of this paper (§
3
–
5
)
is devoted to the combinatorial theory of join trees. We establish tradeoffs between a few “size” and “depth” measures on join trees, which we are able to lift to size-depth tradeoffs for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas by extending the Pathset Framework introduced in papers
[
21
,
23
]
.

The rest of this introduction is organized as follows. §
1.1
introduces iterated matrix multiplication problems
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
.
§
1.2
discusses the formula complexity of these problems in relation to the
\mathit{NC^{1}}
vs.
\mathit{L}
question.
§
1.3
reviews some upper bounds on the
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formula size of
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
. §
1.4
states our main results.
§
1.5
discusses prior related work.
§
1.6
briefly outlines the rest of the paper.

1.1
Iterated matrix multiplication problems

Iterated Matrix Multiplication

(
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
) is the task, given
n\times n
matrices
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(k)}
(over some specified field), of computing the
1,1
-entry of
the matrix product

M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}
.
In algebraic complexity theory,
this problem is
represented by
a formal polynomial of
kn^{2}
variables (the entries of matrices
M^{(i)}
):

\displaystyle\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}(\vec{M})=\sum_{a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1}\,\in\,[n]}\ M^{(1)}_{\smash{1},a_{1}}M^{(2)}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\cdots M^{(k)}_{a_{k-1},\smash{1}}.

Iterated Boolean Matrix Multiplication
(
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
) is
the corresponding problem for matrices with entries in the Boolean ring
\{0,1\}
with addition
\vee
and multiplication
\wedge
. This problem is represented by a monotone Boolean function
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
described by the formula

\displaystyle\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}(\vec{M})=\bigvee_{a_{1},\dots,a_{k-1}\,\in\,[n]}\ M^{(1)}_{\smash{1},a_{1}}\wedge M^{(2)}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\wedge\dots\wedge M^{(k)}_{a_{k-1},\smash{1}}.

A special case of this problem arises when the input
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(k)}
are promised to be
permutation matrices
, or more generally
sub-permutation matrices
with at most a single
1
in each row and column. (For such matrices,
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
coincides with the function computed by
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
over any field.)
These restricted promise problems are called

Iterated (Sub-)Permutation Matrix Multiplication
and denoted by
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
.
Note that there is a significant difference in computational complexity: whereas
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
are complete for nondeterministic logspace
\mathit{NL}
and its algebraic analogue
\mathit{VBP}
(polynomial-size algebraic branching programs), both
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
are complete for deterministic logspace
\mathit{L}

[
8
]
.

This paper focuses on the problem
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
rather than
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
, since like
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
it is naturally represented by a
monotone
Boolean function (whose domain is the downward-closed subset of
\smash{\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}}
representing
k
-tuples of sub-permutation matrices).
It is natural to study the complexity of
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
in both monotone and non-monotone models of computation.
In contrast,
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
is an inherently non-monotone “slice function” whose relevant inputs (
k
-tuples of permutation matrices) have equal Hamming weight
kn
; for such functions, it is well-known that monotone and non-monotone formula complexity coincide up to a polynomial factor
[
6
]
.

1.2
Formula complexity and the question of
\mathit{NC^{1}}
vs.
\mathit{L}

A
formula
is a rooted tree whose leaves (“inputs”) are labeled by constants (
0
or
1
) or literals (
X_{i}
or
\overline{X}_{i}
) and whose non-leaves (“gates”) are labeled by
\bigwedge
or
\bigvee
.
Understanding the formula complexity of iterated matrix multiplication problems is a major challenge at the frontier of lower bounds
in Circuit Complexity.
Recall that
\mathit{NC^{1}}
is — by one of its equivalent definitions — the complexity class of languages that are decidable by a sequence of polynomial-size formulas. It is known that
\mathit{AC^{0}}\subset\mathit{AC^{0}}[\oplus]\subset\mathit{NC^{1}}\subseteq\mathit{L}\subseteq\mathit{NL}\subseteq\mathit{P}\subseteq\mathit{NP}
, but open whether
\mathit{NC^{1}}
is a proper subclass of
\mathit{L}
, or even
\mathit{NP}
.
At present,
no lower bound better than
n^{3-o(1)}
is known on the minimum formula size of any
explicit

sequence of Boolean functions (describing a language in
\mathit{P}
, or even
\mathit{NP}
)
[
27
]
.

Historically, the first lower bounds against many weak circuit classes have targeted functions of barely higher complexity. Consider the classical results that
\textsc{parity}\in\mathit{AC^{0}}[\oplus]\setminus\mathit{AC^{0}}

[
1
,
9
]
and
\textsc{maj}\in\mathit{NC^{1}}\setminus\mathit{AC^{0}}[\oplus]

[
20
,
26
]
. Even the best
n^{3-o(1)}
formula lower bound
[
12
,
27
]
targets a function (Andreev’s function) with
O(n^{3})
size formulas.
From this perspective,
Iterated Sub-Permutation Matrix Multiplication

\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
is the ideal target for a super-polynomial lower bound against
\mathit{NC^{1}}
.
The smallest known formulas for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
have size
n^{O(\log k)}
, which is widely believed to be optimal.
A matching
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
lower bound on the formula size of
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
(respectively:
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
) for any jointly super-constant range of
k
and
n
would separate
\mathit{NC^{1}}
from
\mathit{L}
(respectively:
\mathit{NC^{1}}
from
\mathit{NL}
,
\mathit{VNC^{1}}
from
\mathit{VBP}
).

Motivated by this goal, previous work of the author
[
21
,
23
]

took aim at this lower bound challenge in two restricted classes of formulas:

(+) monotone unbounded-depth formulas, and

(–) non-monotone bounded-depth formulas.

Papers
[
21
,
23
]
established tight
n^{\Omega(k)}
lower bounds in settings (+) and (–) with respect to an average-case version of
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
(on random matrices with i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(\frac{1}{n})
entries).
The following theorem restates these results as lower bounds for problems
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
, which follow from randomized
\mathit{AC^{0}}
reductions from average-case
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
21
,
23
]
)
.

For all
k\leq\log\log n
, size
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
is required by both

(+)
monotone formulas solving
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
,

(–)
formulas of depth
\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{O(1)}}
solving
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
.

Both of these lower bounds come “close” to showing
\mathit{NC^{1}}\neq\mathit{L}
. To separate these complexity classes, it suffices to extend (+) from the monotone function
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
to the slice function
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
. It also suffices to extend (–) from
depth
\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{O(1)}}
to depth
\log n
.

1.3
Upper bounds

Lower bounds (+) and (–) of Theorem
1.1
are simultaneously shown to be tight
by bounded-depth monotone formulas implementing the standard divide-and-conquer (a.k.a. recursive doubling) technique of Savitch
[
25
]
.

Proposition 1.2
.

\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
is solvable by monotone formulas of size
n^{O(\log k)}
and depth
O(\log k)
for all
n
and
k
.

This raises the interesting question: what about formulas of very small depths
2,3,4,\dots
below
O(\log k)
?
Here the best known upper bounds exhibit a typical size-depth tradeoff.
In fact, there are two different tradeoffs given by distinct families of (unbounded fan-in)
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas and (semi-unbounded fan-in)
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas.
1
1

1

See §
2.2
for definitions of classes
\Sigma_{d}
and
\Pi_{d}
of depth-
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas.
Consistently throughout this paper, “
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
” refers to
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas where
\bigwedge
-gates have fan-in
O(k^{1/d})
in the context of
upper bounds
and
O(n^{1/k})
in
lower bounds
.
Note that
k^{1/d}\ll n^{1/k}
for the range of parameters we consider.

Proposition 1.3
.

When
k^{1/d}
is an integer,

\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
is solvable by size
kn^{dk^{1/d}}
monotone formulas of the following types:

(I)

\Sigma_{2d}
formulas with
\textstyle\bigwedge
-fan-in
k^{1/d}

(
and
\textstyle\bigvee
-fan-in
n^{k^{\smash{1/d}}}
)
,

(II)

\Sigma_{d+1}
as well as
\Pi_{d+1}
formulas
(
with fan-in
k^{1/d}n^{k^{\smash{1/d}}}
)
.

To illustrate constructions (I) and (II), Examples
1.4
and
1.5
below concretely present formulas (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{2d}}
, (II)
{}_{\Sigma_{d+1}}
and (II)
{}_{\Pi_{d+1}}
in the cases
(d,k)=(1,5)
and
(d,k)=(2,5^{2})
.

Example 1.4

(The case
d=1
and
k=5
of Proposition
1.3
)
.

For sub-permutation matrices
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(5)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
and indices
a_{0},a_{5}\in[n]
, the
a_{0},a_{5}
-entry of the product
M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(5)}
is computed by the following monotone
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Pi_{2}
(a.k.a. DNF and CNF) formulas:

\displaystyle\bigvee_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}\>

\displaystyle\Big(\ M^{(1)}_{a_{0},a_{1}}\wedge M^{(2)}_{a_{1},a_{2}}\wedge M^{(3)}_{a_{2},a_{3}}\wedge M^{(4)}_{a_{3},a_{4}}\wedge M^{(5)}_{a_{4},a_{5}}\ \Big),

(
\textup{{D}}_{a_{0},a_{5}}
)

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4}}\>

\displaystyle\Big(\ \bigvee_{b_{1}\neq a_{1}}M^{(1)}_{a_{0},\smash{b_{1}}}\vee\bigvee_{b_{2}\neq a_{2}}M^{(2)}_{a_{1},\smash{b_{2}}}\vee\bigvee_{b_{3}\neq a_{3}}M^{(3)}_{a_{2},\smash{b_{3}}}\vee\bigvee_{b_{4}\neq a_{4}}M^{(4)}_{a_{3},\smash{b_{4}}}\vee M^{(5)}_{a_{4},a_{5}}\ \Big).

(
\textup{{C}}_{a_{0},a_{5}}
)

Note that
\textup{{D}}_{a_{0},a_{5}}
computes
(M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(5)})_{a_{0},a_{5}}

for arbitrary Boolean matrices
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(5)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
, whereas
\textup{{C}}_{a_{0},a_{5}}
relies on the assumption that
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(5)}
that have at most a single
1
in each row.
Setting
a_{0}=a_{5}=1
, monotone
\Sigma_{2}
and
\Pi_{2}
formulas
\textup{{D}}_{1,1}
and
\textup{{C}}_{1,1}
compute the function
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,5}
. This provide the case
d=1
and
k=5
of Proposition
1.3
: namely,
\textup{{D}}_{1,1}
furnishes upper bounds (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{2}}
and (II)
{}_{\Sigma_{2}}
, while
\textup{{C}}_{1,1}
furnishes (II)
{}_{\Pi_{2}}
.

Example 1.5

(The case
d=2
and
k=25
of Proposition
1.3
)
.

\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,25}
is computed by the following monotone
\Sigma_{4}
,
\Sigma_{3}
and
\Pi_{3}
formulas, built from sub-formulas
\textup{{D}}_{a_{5(i-1)},a_{5i}}
and
\textup{{C}}_{a_{5(i-1)},a_{5i}}
over matrices
M^{(5i+1)},\dots,M^{(5i)}
:

\displaystyle\bigvee_{a_{5},a_{10},a_{15},a_{20}}\>

\displaystyle\Big(\ \textup{{D}}_{1,a_{5}}\wedge\textup{{D}}_{a_{5},a_{10}}\wedge\textup{{D}}_{a_{10},a_{15}}\wedge\textup{{D}}_{a_{15},a_{20}}\wedge\textup{{D}}_{a_{20},1}\ \Big),

\textup{(I)}_{\Sigma_{4}}

\displaystyle\bigvee_{a_{5},a_{10},a_{15},a_{20}}\>

\displaystyle\Big(\ \textup{{C}}_{1,a_{5}}\wedge\textup{{C}}_{a_{5},a_{10}}\wedge\textup{{C}}_{a_{10},a_{15}}\wedge\textup{{C}}_{a_{15},a_{20}}\wedge\textup{{C}}_{a_{20},1}\ \Big),

\textup{(II)}_{\Sigma_{3}}

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{a_{5},a_{10},a_{15},a_{20}}\>

\displaystyle\Big(\ \bigvee_{b_{5}\neq a_{5}}\textup{{D}}_{1,b_{5}}\ \vee\bigvee_{b_{10}\neq a_{10}}\textup{{D}}_{a_{5},b_{10}}\vee\bigvee_{b_{15}\neq a_{15}}\textup{{D}}_{a_{10},b_{15}}\vee\bigvee_{b_{20}\neq a_{20}}\textup{{D}}_{a_{15},b_{20}}\vee\textup{{D}}_{a_{20},1}\ \Big).

\textup{(II)}_{\Pi_{3}}

Note that (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{4}}
has
\bigwedge
-fan-in
5
(
=k^{1/d}
) and all three formulas have size at most
25n^{10}
(
=kn^{dk^{1/d}}
) as required.
A similar recurrence produces formulas (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{2d}}
, (II)
{}_{\Sigma_{d+1}}
and (II)
{}_{\Pi_{d+1}}
for all
d\geq 3
.

Formulas (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{2d}}
(and their algebraic counterparts with
\sum,\prod
replacing
\bigvee,\bigwedge
) implement a standard divide-and-conquer algorithm for problems
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
. This algorithm recursively partitions the matrix product
{M^{(1)}\cdots M^{(k)}}
as
N^{(1)}\cdots N^{(k^{(d-1)/d})}
where
N^{(i)}
is the sub-product
M^{((i-1)k^{1/d}+1)}\cdots M^{(ik^{1/d})}
.
Formulas (II)
{}_{\Sigma_{d+1}}
and (II)
{}_{\Pi_{d+1}}
for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
utilize a similar recurrence, which generalizes the well-known
\Sigma_{d+1}
and
\Pi_{d+1}
formulas for
\textsc{parity}_{k}
(which are essentially the
n=2
case of formulas (II)).

Notice that Proposition
1.3
implies the
n^{O(\log k)}
upper bound of Proposition
1.2
when
d=\log k
. In contrast, we remark taht the
circuit
versions of constructions (I) and (II) have size
O(kn^{\smash{k^{1/d}}})
, which shrinks to merely polynomial
O(kn^{2})
at
d=\log k
.

1.4
Our tradeoffs for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas

Lower bounds (+) and (–) of Theorem
1.1
were proved using a technique, known as the Pathset Framework, which reduces formulas computing
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}

to simpler combinatorial objects called
join trees
(binary trees of
\cup
-gates which construct the length-
k
path graph from its single-edge subgraphs).
This reduction provides a means of lifting lower bounds for join trees to lower bounds for formulas computing
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
.

A curious feature of lower bound (–) for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas in
[
23
]
is that the reduction to join trees starts by converting
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas an equivalent DeMorgan formulas with fan-in
2
.
The resulting
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
lower bound applies to
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas up to depth
\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{O(1)}}

.
Significantly, the depth
d
does not appear as a parameter in the lower bound, in contrast to typical
\mathit{AC^{0}}
lower bounds that use switching lemmas or the polynomial approximation method in bottom-up fashion.
This is a powerful feature of the result, which is tight above depth
O(\log k)
.
However, the lower bound technique does not (in any obvious way) yield lower bounds stronger than
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
for formulas of depths
2,3,4,\dots
below
O(\log k)
.

The results of this paper expand the Pathset Framework of
[
21
,
23
]
in order to extend
lower bounds
(+) and (–)
of Theorem
1.1
to size-depth tradeoffs for both
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas.
We obtain tradeoffs for monotone formulas which match the upper bounds of Proposition
1.3
.
We are able to extend these tradeoffs to non-monotone formulas, at the expense of a slightly weaker lower bound and smaller range of
k
.

Theorem 1.6

(Size-depth tradeoffs for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas computing
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
)
.

For all
k\leq\log\log n
and
d\leq\log k
, size
n^{\Omega({dk^{1/d}})}
is required by both

I
(I)
+
monotone

\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas of

\smash{\bigwedge}
-depth
d

and

\smash{\bigwedge}
-fan-in
n^{1/k}
, and

(II)
+
monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of
depth
d+1
.

In the non-monotone setting, for all
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
and
d\leq\log k
, size
n^{\Omega(\smash{dk^{1/2d}})}
is required by both

I
(I)
-

\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas of

\smash{\bigwedge}
-depth
d

and

\smash{\bigwedge}
-fan-in
n^{1/k}
, and

(II)
-

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of
depth
d+1
.

Note that the maximum
\bigwedge
-fan-in
n^{1/k}
in
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
lower bounds (I)
+
and (I)
-
exceeds the
\bigwedge
-fan-in
k^{1/d}
of formulas (I)
{}_{\Sigma_{2d}}
of Proposition
1.3
.
Monotone lower bounds (I)
+
and (II)
+
are thus tight up to the
constant in the exponent of
n
,
in light of the upper bounds given by Proposition
1.3
.
Also observe that
\mathit{AC^{0}}
tradeoffs (II)
+
and (II)
-
converge at
d=\log k
to the tight
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
lower bounds (+) and (–) of Theorem
1.1
.

Below we remark on a few corollaries and extensions of Theorem
1.6
.

Tradeoffs for circuits.

Formula
lower bounds
n^{\Omega(\smash{dk^{1/d}})}
and
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}
of Theorem
1.6
directly imply
n^{\Omega(\smash{k^{1/d}})}
and
n^{\Omega(\smash{k^{1/2d}})}
lower bounds for the corresponding
\mathit{(S)}\mathit{AC^{0}}

circuits
. This follows from the observation that every size
s

\mathit{AC^{0}}
circuit of depth
d+1
, or
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
circuit of
\bigwedge
-depth
d
and
\bigwedge
-fan-in
s^{O(1/d)}
, expands to an
\mathit{(S)}\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula with size at most
s^{d+O(1)}
.

The range of
k
.

Examination of the proof in §
7
shows that range
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
of non-monotone lower bounds (I)
-
and (II)
-
can be improved to at least
k\leq 0.99\log\log\log\log n
(see Remark
7.35
). The statement of Theorem
1.6
confines the range to
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
for simplicity sake and to emphasize that we are happy with any super-constant
k
.
With some effort, we believe the range
k\leq\log\log n
of monotone lower bounds (I)
-
and (II)
-
can also be improved somewhat, though probably not to
k=n^{\Omega(1)}
using our methods.

Tradeoff for
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
.

Lower bound (II)
-
on the
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula size

of
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
implies a slightly weaker
n^{\Omega({dk^{\smash{1/(2d+O(1))}}})}
lower bound on the
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula size of the more restricted
Iterated Permutation Matrix Multiplication
problem
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
, whose
\mathit{AC^{0}}
complexity was previously studied in

[
2
,
4
,
5
]
.
This extension of lower bound (II)
-
follows from the existence of a randomized
\mathit{AC^{0}}
reduction from
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
to
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
, using
the method of
[
18
,
10
]
to generate uniform random permutations in
\mathit{AC^{0}}
(see also
[
29
]
).

1.5
Related work

1.5.1
Previous lower bounds

Prior to the results of this paper, the strongest size-depth tradeoff for
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
(or
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
)
was
n^{\vphantom{|}k^{\exp(-O(d))}}
, proved by Beame, Impagliazzo and Pitassi
[
4
]
using a special purpose
switching lemma
.
This tradeoff, which is non-trivial to depth
O(\log\log k)
, improved previous tradeoffs of
[
2
,
5
]
which were only non-trivial to depth
O(\log^{\ast}k)
.

With respect to the significantly more general
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
problem,
Chen, Oliveira, Servedio and Tan
[
7
]
established a nearly tight tradeoff
n^{\Omega((1/d)k^{1/d})}
lower bound for depth-
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
circuits.
This lower bound is non-trivial to depth
O({\scriptstyle\frac{\log k}{\log\log k}})
, and for depths
d=o({\scriptstyle\frac{\log k}{\log\log k}})
it is quantitatively stronger than the
n^{\Omega(k^{1/(2d-1)})}
lower bound implied by Theorem
1.6
for depth-
d

{\mathit{AC^{0}}}
circuits.
However, the result of
[
7
]
is fundamentally a size-depth tradeoff for
Sipser functions
(read-once formulas with a carefully chosen fan-in sequence), combined with a reduction from the
\mathit{NC^{1}}
-complete
sipser
problem to
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
. In contrast to
[
4
]
the present paper, this result says nothing about the complexity of
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
or the questions of
\mathit{NC^{1}}
vs.
\mathit{L}
or
\mathit{NC^{1}}
vs.
\mathit{NL}
.

1.5.2
Different regimes of parameters
n
and
k

Our focus in this paper is on the formula complexity of
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
in the regime where
k
is an arbitrarily slow-growing but super-constant function of
n
.
We have not attempted to optimize the ranges of
k\leq\log\log n
and
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
in Theorem
1.6
.
We remark that the circuit lower bound of Beame
et al

[
4
]
and Chen
et al

[
7
]

extend to larger ranges
k\leq\log n
and
k\leq n^{1/5}
, respectively.

In the opposite parameter regime where
n=2
and
k
is unbounded, all three problems
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}

are equivalent to
\textsc{parity}_{k}
under
\mathit{AC^{0}}
reductions. Here tight
2^{\Omega(k^{1/d})}
and
2^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
lower bounds for depth
d+1

\mathit{AC^{0}}
circuits and formulas computing
\textsc{parity}_{k}
were shown by Håstad
[
11
]
and the author
[
22
]
respectively.

The complexity jumps at
n=5
,
where all three problems are complete for
\mathit{NC^{1}}

by Barrington’s Theorem

[
3
]
.
The complexity of
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
(resp.
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
) then jumps again at
k=n
to being complete for
\mathit{NL}
(resp.
\mathit{L}
).
(See
[
19
,
30
]

concerning
\mathit{TC^{0}}
and
\mathit{ACC^{0}}
circuits for
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
.)

1.5.3
Algebraic circuit classes

The Iterated Matrix Multiplication polynomial (
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
) is computable by set-multilinear formulas of product-depth
d
and size
n^{O(dk^{1/d})}
by construction (I) of Proposition
1.3
with
\sum,\prod
replacing
\bigwedge,\bigvee
.
A recent breakthrough of Limaye, Srinivasan and Tavenas
[
17
]
showed that arithmetic formulas of product-depth
d
computing
\textup{{imm}}_{n,k}
require size
n^{k^{1/\exp(O(d))}}
.
This tradeoff, quantitatively similar to
[
4
]
, is the first super-polynomial lower bound for constant-depth arithmetic formulas.
A related paper of Tavenas, Limaye, and Srinivasan
[
28
]

gives a different

(\log n)^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
lower bound for
set-multilinear formulas.
In the further restricted class of set-multilinear formulas with the
few parse trees
property,
Legarde, Limaye and Srinivasan
[
16
]
established a tight
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
tradeoff.

He and Rossman
[
13
]
studied the complexity of
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
in the hybrid Boolean-algebraic model of
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula that are invariant under a group action of
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
. This is the class of
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas on
kn^{2}
variables (encoding permutation matrices
M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(k)}
) which are syntactically invariant under a certain action of
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
, where the
i
th symmetric group
\mathrm{Sym}(n)
acts by permuting both the columns of
M^{(i)}
and rows of
M^{(i+1)}
.
For instance, construction (II) of Proposition
1.3
produces
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
-invariant
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of depth
d+1
and size
n^{dk^{1/d}\,+\,O(1)}
which solve
\textup{{pmm}}_{k,n}
for all
k\leq n
and
d\leq\log k
.
The paper
[
13
]
proves a sharply matching
n^{dk^{1/d}\,-\,O(1)}
lower bound in the
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
-invariant setting. This lower bound even extends to
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
-invariant
\mathit{TC^{0}}
formulas.

1.5.4
Smallest known formulas for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}

Construction (II) of Proposition
1.3
in the limit
d=\log_{2}k
produces
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
of depth
O(\log k)
and size
n^{\log_{2}k\,+\,O(1)}
. These formulas are moreover
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
-invariant, monotone, and uniform.
However, these formulas are not the smallest known.
For all
k\leq n
,
Rossman
[
23
]
showed that there exist
(non-
\mathrm{Sym}(n)^{k-1}
-invariant, non-monotone, non-uniform)
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas for
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
of depth
O(\log k)
and size
n^{\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}k\,+\,O(1)}
.
This was subsequently improved to
n^{\frac{1}{3}\log_{\varphi}k\,+\,O(1)}
where
\varphi=\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2}
by Kush and Rossman
[
15
]
, who have conjectured
that
\frac{1}{3}\log_{\varphi}k\approx 0.49\log_{2}k
is optimal in the exponent of
n
.

1.6
Outline of the paper

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
§
2
gives several definitions
pertaining to
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}

formulas, subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, and join trees.
§
3
states our combinatorial main result (Theorem
3.6
) consisting of two distinct tradeoffs (I) and (II) for join trees.
Parts (I) and (II) of Theorem
3.6
are proved in the next two sections §
4
and §
5
.
§
6
reviews the Pathset Framework (introduced in papers
[
21
,
23
]
) and establishes new lower bounds for pathset complexity (Theorem
6.11
), which follow from our tradeoffs for join trees.
Finally, our size-depth tradeoffs for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas (Theorem
1.6
) are proved in §
7
via reductions to our lower bounds for pathset complexity.

We advise the reader that §
2
–
5
of this paper may be read a self-contained work dealing exclusively with combinatorics of join trees for the graph
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.
On the other hand, readers mainly interested in the application to Circuit Complexity (Theorem
1.6
) might wish to skip §
4
–
5
(the proof of Theorem
3.6
) on an initial reading, in order to first understand our method of reducing
\mathit{(S)}\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas computing
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
to pathset formulas computing dense relations
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[n]^{k}
and finally to join trees computing the graph
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.

2
Preliminaries

\mathbb{Z}
is the set of integers, and
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
is the set of nonnegative integers. Throughout this paper,
d,k,m,n
represent positive integers, and
h,i,j,l,r,s,t
represent (usually nonnegative) integers.
[i]
stands for the set
\{1,\dots,i\}
. In particular,
[0]
is the empty set
\emptyset
.

\ln(\cdot)
and
\log(\cdot)
denote the base-
\mathrm{e}
and base-2 logarithms.
\log^{\ast}(\cdot)
is the number of times the
\log(\cdot)
function must be iterated until the result is less than
1
.

2.1
Graphs and trees

Graphs
in this paper are
simple graphs without isolated vertices. That is, a graph
G
consists of
sets
V(G)
and
E(G)\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
such that
V(G)=\bigcup_{e\in E(G)}e
.
The empty graph is denoted by the empty set symbol
\emptyset
.
With the exception of the infinite path graph
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, all other graphs considered in this paper are finite.

Trees
in this paper are finite rooted trees.

Binary trees
are trees in which each non-leaf node has exactly two children, designed “left” and “right”.
For binary trees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
, we denote by
\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
the binary tree consisting of a root with
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
as its left and right subtrees.

2.2
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas

An
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
on variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
is a rooted tree whose leaves (called “inputs”) are labeled by a constant
0
or
1
or literals
X_{i}
or
\overline{X}_{i}
, and whose non-leaves (called “gates”) are labeled by
\bigwedge
or
\bigvee
.
An
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula is
monotone
if no input is labeled by a negative literal
\overline{X}_{i}
.
Every [monotone]
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula computes a [monotone] Boolean function
\{0,1\}^{N}\to\{0,1\}
in the usual way.

We measure the
size
of an
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula by the number of leaves that are labeled by literals.

Depth
(resp.
\bigwedge
-depth
) is the maximum number of gates (resp.
\bigwedge
-gates) on a root-to-leaf-branch.

Fan-in
(resp.
\bigwedge
-fan-in
) is the maximum number of children of any gate (resp.
\bigwedge
-gate).

Depth
0

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas (i.e., literals) are also known as
\Sigma_{0}
formulas
and
\Pi_{0}
formulas
. For
d\geq 1
, a
\Sigma_{d}
formula
(resp.
\Pi_{d}
formula
) is either a
\Pi_{d-1}
formula (resp.
\Sigma_{d-1}
formula) or a depth
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula whose output gate is labeled
\bigvee
(resp.
\bigwedge
). Note that
\Sigma_{2d+1}
formulas are precisely the class of
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas with
\bigwedge
-depth
d
.

\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas
(where “
S
” stands for “semi-unbounded fan-in”) are
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas with bounded
\bigwedge
-fan-in. This usually refers to
\bigwedge
-fan-in
2
(i.e., formulas with unbounded fan-in
\bigvee
gates and fan-in
2

\wedge
gates). However,
when speaking of problems
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
and
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
,
we allow

\bigwedge
-fan-in
O(k^{1/d})
in the context of upper bounds, and at most
n^{1/k}
in the context of lower bounds. Note that
k^{1/d}\ll n^{1/k}
in the regime of parameters that we consider (i.e.,
k\leq\log\log n
and
d\leq\log k
).

2.3
Subgraphs of paths

The graphs that we will mainly consider are
disjoint unions of paths
. It is convenient to regard these as finite subgraphs of the infinite path graph
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with vertex set
\mathbb{Z}
and edge set
\{\{i-1,i\}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

For integers
s<t
, let
\mathsf{Path}_{s,t}
denote subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with vertex set
\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:s\leq i\leq t\}
and edge set
\{\{i-1,i\}\in\mathbb{Z}:s<i\leq t\}
.
For positive integers
k
, we write
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
for
\mathsf{Path}_{0,k}
.

Note that every finite subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
is
is a union of paths
\mathsf{Path}_{s_{1},t_{1}}\cup\dots\cup\mathsf{Path}_{s_{c},t_{c}}
for unique integers
c\geq 0
and
s_{1}<t_{1}<s_{2}<\dots<t_{c-1}<s_{c}<t_{c}
.

The next three definitions introduce notation that will be heavily used throughout the paper.

Definition 2.1

(Parameters
\|G\|
,
\Delta(G)
,
\lambda(G)
)
.

For a finite graph
G\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

\displaystyle\|G\|

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\#\{\textup{edges of }G\},

\displaystyle\vphantom{\bigg|}\Delta(G)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\#\{\textup{connected components of }G\}

\displaystyle=\#\{\textup{vertices of }G\}-\#\{\textup{edges of }G\},

\displaystyle\lambda(G)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{\textup{connected components $C$ of }G}\,\|C\|

\displaystyle=\textup{length of the longest path in }G.

Note that if
G=\mathsf{Path}_{s_{1},t_{1}}\cup\dots\cup\mathsf{Path}_{s_{c},t_{c}}
where
s_{1}<t_{1}<s_{2}<\dots<t_{c-1}<s_{c}<t_{c}
, then

\displaystyle\|G\|=\sum_{i=1}^{c}\ (t_{i}-s_{i}),\qquad\Delta(G)=c,\qquad\lambda(G)=\max_{i\in\{1,\dots,c\}}\ (t_{i}-s_{i}).

Definition 2.2

(The operation
G\ominus F
)
.

For graphs
G,F\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote by
G\ominus F
the subgraph of
G
comprised of the connected components of
G
that are vertex-disjoint from
F
.

Definition 2.3
.

For a sequence of finite graphs
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, let

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern \displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{j\in[m]}\Delta(G_{j}\ominus(G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{j-1})),

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern For
F\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we also introduce a convenient “conditional

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

” notation:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\mid F)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{j\in[m]}\Delta(G_{j}\ominus(F\cup G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{j-1})).

The value of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
is sensitive to the order of
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
, as the following example shows.

Example 2.4
.

Let
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},\dots,E_{25}
be the single-edge subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{25}
in the standard order. We have

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},\dots,E_{25})=1,

since
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern for

.
However, we can increase the

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value by permuting the sequence
E_{1},\dots,E_{25}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(\underbrace{E_{1},E_{3},E_{5},\dots,E_{25}},\,\underbrace{E_{2},E_{4},E_{6},\dots,E_{24}})=13.

It is not hard to check that
13
is the maximum possible

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value among all permutations of
E_{1},\dots,E_{25}
.
Generalizing this example from
25
to any
k
, we get a maximum

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value of
\lceil k/2\rceil
.

For later reference, the following lemma records some basic properties of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern , which follow
directly
from

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
F,G_{1},\dots,G_{m},H_{1},\dots,H_{m}
be finite subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

(a)

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern (e)

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\,G_{1}\cup G_{2},\,G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3},\,\dots,\,G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{m})
.
∎

2.4
Join trees

We define the notion of
join trees
with respect to arbitrary finite graphs
G
. However, our focus will soon narrow to join trees for subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.

Definition 2.6

(Join tree)
.

For any finite graph
G
, a
G
-join tree
is a
binary tree
T
together a labeling of nodes of
T
by subgraphs of
G
such that

•

each leaf is labeled by a single-edge subgraph of
G
or the empty graph,

•

each non-leaf is labeled by the union of the graphs labeling its children, and

•

the root is labeled by
G
.

Note that the labeling function is induced by its value on leaves of
T
, given by a surjective partial function from
\{
leaves of
T\}
to
E(G)
.

We consider non-standard notions of “depth” and “size” for join trees. Our combinatorial main theorem gives tradeoffs between these parameters, which we define next.

Definition 2.7

(Operations
\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
,
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
and
\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rrbracket
)
.

If
T_{1}
is a
G_{1}
-join tree and
T_{2}
is a
G_{2}
-join tree, then we denote by
\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
the
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
-join tree consisting of a root with left and right subtrees
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
with the naturally induced labeling of nodes.

Operations
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\cdot\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
are defined inductively on finite sequences of join trees. In the base case
m=1
, we have
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle=\llbracket T_{1}\rrbracket\vcentcolon=T_{1}
.
For
m\geq 2
, we define

\displaystyle\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\langle T_{1},\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{2},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle T_{1},\langle T_{2},\ldots\langle T_{m-2},\langle T_{m-1},T_{m}\rangle\rangle\ldots\rangle\rangle,\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rrbracket

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\langle\,\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m-1}\rrbracket,\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m-2},T_{m}\rrbracket\,\rangle\vphantom{\big|}\quad

\displaystyle=\llbracket\,\langle T_{1},T_{m}\rangle,\langle T_{2},T_{m}\rangle,\dots,\langle T_{m-1},T_{m}\rangle\,\rrbracket.\vphantom{\Big|}

Note that
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle=\llbracket T_{1},T_{2}\rrbracket=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
in the case
m=2
. However, note that operations
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\cdot\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
and
\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket
are different for
m\geq 3
. For visual reference, the case
m=5
is picture below where
T_{1},\dots,T_{5}
are
G_{1},\dots,G_{5}
-join trees:

\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4},T_{5}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle:
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}\cup G_{4}\cup G_{5}
G_{2}\cup G_{3}\cup G_{4}\cup G_{5}
G_{3}\cup G_{4}\cup G_{5}
G_{4}\cup G_{5}
T_{1}
T_{2}
T_{3}
T_{4}
T_{5}

\llbracket T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4},T_{5}\rrbracket:
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}\cup G_{4}\cup G_{5}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}\cup G_{4}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}\cup G_{5}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{4}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{3}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}\cup G_{5}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
G_{1}\cup G_{3}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
G_{1}\cup G_{4}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
G_{1}\cup G_{3}
G_{1}\cup G_{2}
G_{1}\cup G_{5}

T_{1}
T_{2}

T_{4}
T_{1}
T_{2}
T_{1}
T_{1}
T_{3}
T_{1}
T_{2}
T_{1}
T_{5}

Definition 2.8

(Depth measures)
.

We define

\langle\hskip 0.59998pt\rangle
-depth
,
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
as the pointwise minimum functions
\{
join trees
\}\to\{0,1,2,\dots\}
satisfying

\displaystyle 0pt_{\langle\hskip 0.49164pt\rangle}(\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\max\{0pt_{\langle\hskip 0.49164pt\rangle}(T_{1}),\,0pt_{\langle\hskip 0.49164pt\rangle}(T_{2})\},

\displaystyle 0pt_{\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle}(\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\max\{0pt_{\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle}(T_{1}),\,\dots,\,0pt_{\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle}(T_{m})\},\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle 0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rrbracket)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\max\{0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(T_{1}),\,\dots,\,0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(T_{m})\}.

That is, the
\langle\hskip 0.59998pt\rangle
-depth (resp.
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
,
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth) of
T
is the minimum nesting depth of binary
{\cup}
operations (resp. unbounded
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
,
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
operations) required to express
T
in terms of individual edges.

\langle\hskip 0.59998pt\rangle
-depth is the standard notion of “depth” for binary trees, that is, the maximum length (= number of parent-to-child descents) of a root-to-leaf branch.

\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth is what might be called “left-depth”, that is, the maximum number of left descents on a root-to-leaf-branch.

\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth is perhaps a new notion, which arises in connection to our tradeoffs for unbounded fan-in
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas.

In order to define our “size” measure for join trees, we first introduce the notion of
branch coverings
.

Definition 2.9

(Branch coverings)
.

Let
T
be a
G
-join tree. We associate each root-to-leaf branch
(b_{1},\dots,b_{\ell})
in
T
(where
b_{1}
is the root and
b_{\ell}
is a leaf) with the sequence of graphs
B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}
where

•

B_{j}
labels the sibling of
b_{j+1}
(i.e., the opposite-side child of
b_{j}
) for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell-1\}
,
and

•

B_{\ell}
labels the leaf
b_{\ell}
(note that
\|B_{\ell}\|\leq 1
).

Note that
B_{1}\cup\dots\cup B_{\ell}=G
, that is,
B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}
is a covering of
G
.
We refer to sets
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}\}
arising from root-to-leaf branches as
T
-branch coverings
of
G
.

One standard way of measuring the “size” of a binary tree is by the number of leaves (
=
number of root-to-leaf branches). For join trees
T
with graph
G\subset\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, we consider a completely different “size” measure defined as the
maximum
of a certain complexity measure over all
T
-branch coverings
\{B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}\}
of
G
. This complexity measure is itself the
maximum

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value among all orderings
B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(\ell)}
.
This rather complicated “size” measure, denoted by
\Psi
, arises naturally in the context of
pathset complexity
(see §
6
and in particular Corollary
6.7
and Lemma
6.10
, which explain the roles of
\vec{\Delta}
and
\Psi
).

Definition 2.10

(
\Psi
-size of join trees)
.

Let
T
be a
G
-join tree where
G\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
. We define the
\Psi
-size
of
T
by

\displaystyle\Psi(T)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textup{$\vphantom{\Big|}T$-branch coverings $\{B_{1},\dots,B_{\ell}\}$ of $G$}\\
\textup{and permutations $\smash{\pi:[\ell]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[\ell]}$}\end{subarray}}\ \mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(\ell)}).

Stated differently,
\Psi(T)
is the maximum value of
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(C_{1},\dots,C_{m})
over all
T
-branch covering
\mathcal{C}
of
G
and enumerations
C_{1},\dots,C_{m}
of
\mathcal{C}
.
This alternative description of
\Psi(T)
emphasizes the fact that we treat
T
-branch coverings as (unordered) sets.

3
Tradeoffs for join trees

In this section we state the combinatorial main theorem of this paper, Theorem
3.6
, which gives tight tradeoffs between the
\Psi
-size and the
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
- and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join trees.
We also state two auxiliary results, Lemmas
3.4
and
3.5
, which concern coverings of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.
Proofs of these results are postponed to §
4
and §
5
(the combinatorial core of the paper, which may be read independently of §
6
and §
7
).
We also state and prove a key numerical inequality, Lemma
3.9
, which will be used later on in the inductive proofs of our tradeoffs for join trees.

3.1
Shift permutations

Given a sequence
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\subset\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we will be interested in the question of maximizing
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{\pi(1)},\dots,G_{\pi(m)})
over permutations
\pi
.
We will also be studying a variant of this question over a restricted class of permutations, which we call “shift permutations”. (The author is unaware of any standard terminology for this class of permutations.)

Definition 3.1
.

A
shift permutation
is a permutation
\sigma:[m]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[m]
satisfying
\sigma(j)\geq j-1
for all
j\in[m]
.
(For clarity of notation, we will consistently write
\sigma
for shift permutations and
\pi
for general permutations.)

There are exactly
2^{m-1}
shift permutations, which we will index via a bijection

\displaystyle I\mapsto\sigma_{I}:\{\textup{subsets of $[m]$ containing }m\}\to\{\textup{shift permutations}\}.

Suppose
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}\}
with
0=:i_{0}\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{p}=m
. Then we define

\sigma_{I}:[m]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[m]
by

\displaystyle\sigma_{I}:(1,\dots,m)\mapsto(\underbrace{i_{1},1,2,\dots,i_{1}-1}_{\textup{first }i_{1}},\,\underbrace{i_{2},i_{1}+1,i_{2}+2\dots,i_{2}-1}_{\textup{next }i_{2}-i_{1}},\,\dots,\,\underbrace{i_{p},i_{p-1}+1,i_{p-1}+2,\dots,m-1}_{\textup{last }m-i_{p-1}}).

That is,

\displaystyle\sigma_{I}(j)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}i_{h}&\text{if }j=i_{h-1}+1\text{ for some }h\in[p],\\
j-1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Note that
\sigma_{[m]}
is the identity permutation and
\sigma_{\{m\}}
is the
m
-cycle
(1,\dots,m)\mapsto(m,1,\dots,m-1)
.
Also note that inverse of the bijection
I\mapsto\sigma_{I}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma\mapsto\Big\{j\in[m]:\{\sigma(1),\sigma(2),\dots,\sigma(j)\}=\{1,2,\dots,j\}\Big\}\quad\Big({=}\ \{\sigma(j):j\in[m]\text{ such that }\sigma(j)\geq j\}\Big).

Example 3.2
.

Again consider the sequence
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},\dots,E_{25}
of the single-edge subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{25}
.
Example
2.4
observes that
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(E_{\pi(1)},\dots,E_{\pi(25)})=13
where
\pi
is the permutation

\displaystyle\pi:(1,\dots,25)\mapsto(\underbrace{1,3,5,\dots,25},\,\underbrace{2,4,6,\dots,24}).

Note that
\pi
is
not
a shift permutation.
However, we can achieve the same value
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern via the shift permutation

.
That is, we have

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value of
\frac{k+1}{2}
.

Example 3.3
.

Suppose that instead of
E_{1},\dots,E_{25}
in the usual order, we are given the sequence

\displaystyle\underbrace{E_{1},E_{6},E_{11},E_{16},E_{21}},\,\underbrace{E_{2},E_{7},E_{12},E_{17},E_{22}},\,\underbrace{E_{3},E_{8},E_{13},E_{18},E_{23}},\,\underbrace{E_{4},E_{9},E_{14},E_{19},E_{24}},\,\underbrace{E_{5},E_{10},E_{15},E_{20},E_{25}}.

Note that the

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value of this sequence equals
5
.

What is the best

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value we can obtain by applying a shift permutation to this sequence of graphs? It turns out that the answer is
7
. Among many possibilities, this is achieved by the shift permutation
\sigma_{\{15,25\}}
, which produces the sequence

\displaystyle E_{23},\underbrace{E_{1},E_{6},E_{11},E_{16},E_{21}},\,\underbrace{E_{2},E_{7},E_{12},E_{17},E_{22}},\,\underbrace{E_{3},E_{8},E_{13},E_{18}},E_{25},\underbrace{E_{4},E_{9},E_{14},E_{19},E_{24}},\,\underbrace{E_{5},E_{10},E_{15},E_{20}}.

Generalizing this example from
25
to any odd square
k
, we get an ordering
E_{1},E_{\smash{\sqrt{k}}+1},E_{2\smash{\sqrt{k}}+1},\dots,E_{k}
where applying the best shift permutation increases the

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-value from
\sqrt{k}
to
\sqrt{k}+\frac{\sqrt{k}-1}{2}
.

3.2
Combinatorial results of this paper

We now state the main combinatorial results of this paper, whose proofs are given in §
4
and §
5
.
Parts (I) and (II) in the lemmas and theorem below yield lower bounds which match upper bounds (I) and (II) of Proposition
1.3
.

Lemma 3.5

(Main Lemma)
.

Suppose that
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{m}=\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.

(I)

There exists a permutation
\pi

such that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{\pi(1)},\dots,G_{\pi(m)})\geq k/30.

(II)

There exists a shift permutation
\sigma

s.t.
\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{\sigma(1)},\dots,G_{\sigma(m)})\geq\sqrt{k/8}.

Lemma
3.4
is used to prove Lemma
3.5
, which is in turn used to prove the main combinatorial result of this paper:

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
T
be any
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree.

(I)

\displaystyle\Psi(T)\geq\frac{1}{30\mathrm{e}}dk^{1/d}-d

where
d
is the
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth of
T
.

(II)

\displaystyle\Psi(T)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{32\mathrm{e}}}dk^{1/2d}-d

where
d
is the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of
T
.

Note that inequalities (I) and (II) of Theorem
3.6
are respectively
\Omega(dk^{1/d})
and
\Omega(dk^{1/2d})
up to some
d=O(\log k)
, yet
both inequalities become trivial for some slightly larger
d=\Omega(\log k)
.
However, this doesn’t bother us since a different bound
\Phi(T)\geq 0.35\log k
is known by a result of
[
15
]
for different parameter
\Phi(T)
satisfying
\Phi(T)\geq\Psi(T)
.
As we describe later in Corollary
6.12
, we end up with tradeoffs
n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}
and
n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/2d}-1))}
for pathset complexity, which are tight for all
d
. In particular, these tradeoffs both converge to
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
as
d\to\infty
.

3.3
Matching upper bounds

The following
Examples
3.7
and
3.8
show that
the lower bounds of Theorem
3.6
are tight up to a constant factor.

Example 3.7

(Tightness of Theorem
3.6
(I))
.

Let
P
be a path of length
k
, where we assume
\ell\vcentcolon=k^{1/d}
is an integer. Consider the covering
P_{1}\cup P_{2}\cup\dots\cup P_{\ell}=P
where
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\ell}
are consecutive edge-disjoint paths of length
k/\ell
(
=k^{(d-1)/d}
).

Let
T=\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},T_{2},\dots,T_{k}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
be the
P
-join tree of
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
d
where each
T_{i}
is the
P_{i}
-join tree of
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
d-1
constructed recursively by the same process.
Note that
\Psi(T)\leq\ell/2+\max_{i}\Psi(T_{i})
. It follows by induction that
\Psi(T)\leq d\ell/2=dk^{1/d}/2
.

Example 3.8

(Tightness of Theorem
3.6
(II))
.

Let
P
be a path of length
k
, where we assume
\ell\vcentcolon=k^{1/2d}
is an integer.
Consider the covering
\bigcup_{(i,j)\in[\ell]^{2}}P_{i,j}=P
where

\displaystyle\underbrace{P_{1,1},P_{1,2},\dots,P_{1,\ell}},\ \underbrace{P_{2,1},P_{2,2},\dots,P_{2,\ell}},\ \dots,\ \underbrace{P_{\ell,1},P_{\ell,2},\dots,P_{\ell,\ell}}

are consecutive edge-disjoint paths of length
k/\ell^{2}
(
=k^{(d-1)/d}
). Note that
P_{i,1}\cup P_{i,2}\cup\dots\cup P_{i,\ell}
is a path of length
k/\ell
, whilel
P_{1,j}\cup P_{2,j}\cup\dots\cup P_{\ell,j}
is a vertex-disjoint union of
\ell
paths of length
k/\ell^{2}
.

Let

\displaystyle T=\llbracket\underbrace{T_{1,1},T_{2,1},\dots,T_{\ell,1}},\ \underbrace{T_{1,2},T_{2,2},\dots,T_{\ell,2}},\ \dots,\ \underbrace{T_{1,\ell},T_{2,\ell},\dots,T_{\ell,\ell}}\rrbracket

be the
P
-join tree of
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
d
where each
T_{i,j}
is the
P_{i,j}
-join tree of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
d-1
constructed recursively by the same process.
Note that
\Psi(T)\leq 2\ell+\max_{i,j}\Psi(T_{i,j})
. It follows by induction that
\Psi(T)\leq 2d\ell=2dk^{1/2d}
.

3.4
Numerical inequality used in the induction

Both inequalities of Theorem
3.6
are proved by induction on the parameter
d
. The following numerical inequality plays a key role in the induction step.

Lemma 3.9
.

For all real numbers
x_{1},\dots,x_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}\geq 0
and
d>1
,

\displaystyle\max_{j\in\{1,\dots,m\}}\Bigg((d-1)x_{j}^{1/(d-1)}+\sum_{i=1}^{j}y_{i}\Bigg)\geq d\Bigg(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}y_{j}\Bigg)^{1/d}.

Proof.

Let

\displaystyle\vphantom{\big|}Y_{j}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sum_{i=1}^{j}y_{i}\quad\text{ for $j\in\{0,\dots,m\}$},

\displaystyle\vphantom{\big|}Z

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=x_{1}y_{1}+\dots+x_{m}y_{m}.

Assume that
Z>0
, since otherwise the inequality is trivially valid.

Toward a contradiction, assume that for all
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, we have

\displaystyle(d-1)x_{j}^{1/(d-1)}+Y_{j}<d\left(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\right)^{1/d}.

It follows that

\displaystyle x_{j}y_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}-Y_{j}\bigg)^{d-1}y_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\int_{Y_{j-1}}^{Y_{j}}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}-Y_{j}\bigg)^{d-1}\ \mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\int_{Y_{j-1}}^{Y_{j}}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}-w\bigg)^{d-1}\ \mathrm{d}w.

We now get a contradiction
Z<Z
as follows:

\displaystyle Z=\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}y_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{Y_{m}}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}-w\bigg)^{d-1}\ \mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\int_{0}^{d(Z/\mathrm{e})^{1/d}}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}-w\bigg)^{d-1}\ \mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\text{(since $Y_{m}\leq d(Z/\mathrm{e})^{1/d}$ by}\\
&\text{\ case $j=m$ of our assumption)}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(d-1)^{d-1}}\frac{1}{d}\bigg(d\bigg(\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}\bigg)^{1/d}\bigg)^{d}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\text{(since $\textstyle\int_{0}^{C}(C-w)^{d-1}\>\mathrm{d}w=\frac{1}{d}C^{1/d}$}\\
&\text{ for $C\geq 0$)}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\bigg(1+\frac{1}{d-1}\bigg)^{d-1}\frac{Z}{\mathrm{e}}

\displaystyle<Z

\displaystyle\text{(since $1+C\leq\exp(C)$ for $C\geq 0$).}\qed

Lemma
3.9
is invoked once and twice, respectively, in the induction step of lower bounds (I) and (II) of Theorem
3.6
. This accounts for the different values
1/d
vs.
1/2d
in the exponent of
k
in these two bounds.

We remark that in the simplest case
m=1
of Lemma
3.9
, one can show a stronger bound (without the constant
1/\mathrm{e}
) by elementary calculus.
We record this claim as a separate lemma, since we will use it later on (as one of the two applications of Lemma
3.9
in the proof of Theorem
3.6
(II)).

Lemma 3.10

(The
m=1
case of Lemma
3.9
)
.

For all real numbers
x,y\geq 0
and
d>1
, we have

\displaystyle(d-1)x^{1/(d-1)}+y

\displaystyle\geq d(xy)^{1/d}.

We remark that Lemma
3.10
is a common inequality that shows up in formula size-depth tradeoffs including
[
16
,
22
]
.
We suspect that the more general inequality given by Lemma
3.9

might find applications beyond the present paper to additional formula tradeoffs which involve the function
dk^{1/d}
.

4
Join tree tradeoff (I)

In this section we prove inequalities (I) of Lemmas
3.4
–
3.5
and Theorem
3.6
, which we eventually use to prove tradeoffs (I)
+
and (I)
-
of Theorem
1.6
for monotone and non-monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
circuits.

4.1
Proof of Pre-Main Lemma
3.4
(I)

The following notion of

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-greedy sequences of subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
plays a key role in our proof of Lemma
3.4
(I).

Definition 4.1

(

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil

-greedy sequence)
.

For finite subgraphs
F
and
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we say that the sequence
(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
is
