where the last inequality is by Lemma
5.8
(i).
∎

5.4
Proof of Theorem
3.6
(II)

As in §
4.3
, we consider a more general restatement of Theorem
3.6
(II) that is better suitable for induction on
d
.

Main Theorem
3.6
(II)

(slightly more general restatement)
.

Let
P
be a finite subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
T
be a
P
-join tree of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
d
.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi(T)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{32\mathrm{e}}}d\lambda(P)^{1/2d}-d+\Delta(P).

Proof.

We argue by induction on
d
. Here we treat
d=0
as the base case, where
P
is a single edge (or empty) and the bound
\Psi(T)\geq\Delta(P)
is trivial.

For the induction step where
d\geq 1
, we reduce to the case
\Delta(P)=1
exactly as in our
proof of Theorem
3.6
(I). We therefore assume that
T=\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rrbracket
is a
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
m
and aim to show

\Psi(T)\geq\varepsilon dk^{1/2d}-d+1\quad\text{ where }\quad\varepsilon\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{32\mathrm{e}}}.

(36)

Let
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{m}=\mathsf{Path}_{k}
where
G_{j}
is the root graph of
T_{j}
, and let
g\vcentcolon=\mathrm{gap}(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
. We prove inequality (
36
) by considering three cases, according to whether
\max_{j\in[m]}\lambda(G_{j})
is:

greater than
\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})}
,
between
\dfrac{g}{8}
and
\dfrac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})}
,
or less than
\dfrac{g}{8}
.

The most interesting is the third case, which relies on Lemma
5.10
(the stronger version of Main Lemma
3.5
(II)).

Case 1:

Assume there exists
j\in[m]
such that

\lambda(G_{j})\geq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})}
.

By the induction hypothesis applied to
T_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi(T_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon(d-1)\lambda(G_{j})^{1/(2d-2)}+\Delta(G_{j})-d+1

(37)

\displaystyle=(2d-2)\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G^{j})\right)^{1/(2d-2)}+\Delta(G_{j})-d+1.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Psi(T)+d-1{}_{\vphantom{\big|}}

\displaystyle\geq\Psi(T_{j})+d-1

(since clearly
\Psi(T)\geq\Psi(T_{j})
)

\displaystyle\geq(2d-2)\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\right)^{1/(2d-2)}+\Delta(G_{j})

(by induction hypothesis (
37
))

\displaystyle\geq(2d-1)\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\frac{\Delta(G_{j})}{2}\right)^{1/(2d-1)}+\frac{\Delta(G_{j})}{2}

(by Lemma
3.10
)

\displaystyle\geq 2d\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\frac{\Delta(G_{j})^{2}}{4}\right)^{1/2d}

(by Lemma
3.10
again)

\displaystyle\geq 2d\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{4}k\right)^{1/2d}

(since
\lambda(G_{j})\geq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})}\geq\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})^{2}}
)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon dk^{1/2d}.

Case 2:

Assume there exists
j\in[m]
such that

\displaystyle\frac{g}{8}\leq\lambda(G_{j})\leq\frac{\varepsilon^{2}k}{\Delta(G_{j})}.

We have

\displaystyle\Psi(T)+d-1

\displaystyle\geq\Psi(T_{j})+\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\dots,G_{j-1},G_{j+1},\dots,G_{m}\mid G_{j})+d-1

(by
Corollary
5.5
)

\displaystyle\geq(2d-2)\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\right)^{1/(2d-2)}+\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{j},G_{1},\dots,G_{j-1},G_{j+1},\dots,G_{m})\hskip-144.54pt

(by induction hypothesis (
37
))

\displaystyle\geq(2d-2)\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\right)^{1/(2d-2)}+\frac{(1-\varepsilon^{2})k}{4g}

(by
Lemma
5.8
(iii))

\displaystyle\geq 2d\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\lambda(G_{j})\left(\frac{(1-\varepsilon^{2})k}{8g}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2d}

(by
Lemma
3.10
twice)

\displaystyle\geq 2d\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{2d-2}\frac{(1-\varepsilon^{2})^{2}}{64}k\right)^{1/2d}

(since
\lambda(G_{j})\geq\frac{g}{8}
and
k\geq g
)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon d\left(\frac{(1-\varepsilon^{2})^{2}}{16\varepsilon^{2}}k\right)^{1/2d}

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon dk^{1/2d}

\displaystyle\text{(since $\varepsilon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{32\mathrm{e}}}$)}.

Case 3:

Assume
\displaystyle\lambda(G_{j})\leq\frac{g}{8}

for all
j\in[m]
.

By Lemma
5.10
(the stronger version of Main Lemma
3.5
(II)),
there exists a shift permutation
\sigma:[m]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[m]
such that

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\lambda\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern \displaystyle\geq\frac{g-3\max_{j\in[m]}\lambda(G_{j})}{4}>\frac{g}{8},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern \displaystyle\geq\frac{k}{4g}\quad\text{ for all }j\in[m].

\displaystyle j^{\star}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\sigma^{-1}(j),\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=G_{j}\ominus F_{j},\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=T_{j}\ominus F_{j}

\displaystyle({=}\ T_{j}\textup{ restricted to }G^{j}).

\displaystyle\text{(by Lemma \ref{la:numerical0})}.

6
Tradeoffs for pathsets

In this section, we review the Pathset Framework of papers
[
15
,
21
,
23
,
24
]
and prove new tradeoffs for pathset complexity (Theorem
6.11
) that follow from our tradeoffs for join trees (Theorem
3.6
).

6.1
Relations and joins

Throughout this section, we fix arbitrary positive integers
k
and
n
. We additionally fix an arbitrary parameter
\tilde{n}\leq n
. In our application, we will set
\tilde{n}\vcentcolon=n^{(k-1)/k}
.

Definition 6.1

(
G
-relations)
.

For a graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, we refer to sets
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[n]^{V(G)}
as
G
-relations
. We denote the set of all
G
-relations by
\mathscr{R}_{G}
. (That is,
\mathscr{R}_{G}
is the set of “
V(G)
-ary” relations on
[n]
.)

The join operation
\bowtie
combines a
G
-relation and an
H
-relation into a
G\cup H
-relation.

Definition 6.2

(Join)
.

For graphs
G,H\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and relations
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{R}_{G}
and
\mathcal{B}\in\mathscr{R}_{H}
, the
join
of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
is the relation
\mathcal{A}\bowtie\mathcal{B}\in\mathscr{R}_{G\cup H}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\bowtie\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\gamma\in[n]^{V(G)\cup V(H)}:\gamma_{V(G)}\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\gamma_{V(H)}\in\mathcal{B}\}.

Note that the join behaves as direct product
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}
when
V(G)\cap V(H)=\emptyset
, and as intersection
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
when
V(G)=V(H)
.

6.2
Density and pathsets

Definition 6.3

(Density)
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
. The
density
of a relation
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{R}_{G}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{|\mathcal{A}|}{n^{|V(G)|}}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}}[\ \alpha\in\mathcal{A}\ ].

For a graph
F\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and
I=V(F)\cap V(G)
,
the
maximum density of
\mathcal{A}
conditioned on
F

is defined by

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}\mid F)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\max_{\beta\in[n]^{V(F)}}\frac{|\{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}:\alpha_{I}=\beta_{I}\}|}{n^{|V(G)\setminus V(F)|}}

\displaystyle=\max_{\beta\in[n]^{V(F)}}\ \Pr_{\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}}[\ \alpha\in\mathcal{A}\ |\ \alpha_{I}=\beta_{I}\ ].

(Note that
0\leq\mu(\mathcal{A})=\mu(\mathcal{A}\mid\emptyset)\leq\mu(\mathcal{A}\mid F)\leq 1
.)

The next lemma and corollary relate the density of a join to the maximum conditional densities of the constituent relations. The proof is another simple exercise in relational algebra.

Lemma 6.4

(Chain rule for density of a join)
.

For all graphs
F,G,H\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and relations
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{R}_{G}
and
\mathcal{B}\in\mathscr{R}_{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}\bowtie\mathcal{B}\mid F)

\displaystyle\leq\mu(\mathcal{A}\mid F)\cdot\mu(\mathcal{B}\mid F\cup G).

Corollary 6.5

(
m
-ary version of Lemma
6.4
)
.

For all graphs
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and relations
\mathcal{A}_{j}\in\mathscr{R}_{G_{j}}

(
j\in[m]
)
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\>\mu(\mathcal{A}_{j}\mid G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{j-1}).

Furthermore, since the density of the join does not depend on the ordering of relations
\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{m}
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{j=1}^{m}\>\mu(\mathcal{A}_{\pi(j)}\mid G_{\pi(1)}\cup\dots\cup G_{\pi(j-1)})

for every permutation
\smash{\pi:[m]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[m]}
.

The lifting technique of papers
[
21
,
23
]
focuses on a special class of
G
-relations that are subject to a family of density constraints in terms of
\Delta(\cdot|\cdot)
.

Definition 6.6

(
G
-pathsets)
.

For a graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, a
G
-pathset

(with respect to parameters
n
and
\tilde{n}
)
is a relation
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{R}_{G}
such that for all graphs
F\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}\mid F)\leq(1/\tilde{n})^{\Delta(G\,\mid\,F)}.

The set of
G
-pathsets is denoted by
\mathscr{P}_{G}
. (Note that
\mathscr{P}_{G}
is a proper subset of
\mathscr{R}_{G}
when
G
is nonempty.)

The next result (Corollary
6.5
applied to pathsets) bounds the density of a join of pathsets in terms of the operation
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(\cdot)
.

Corollary 6.7
.

For all graphs
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and pathsets
\mathcal{A}_{j}\in\mathscr{P}_{G_{j}}

(
j\in[m]
)
, we have

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A}_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{m})

\displaystyle\leq\,\min_{\pi\,:\,[m]\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\,[m]}\>(1/\tilde{n})^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{\pi(1)},\dots,G_{\pi(m)})}.

6.3
Pathset complexity

For a graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, we define a family of complexity measures
\mu_{T}:\mathscr{P}_{G}\to\mathbb{N}
for each
G
-join tree
T
.

Definition 6.8

(Pathset complexity)
.

Let
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, let
\mathcal{A}\in\mathscr{P}_{G}
, and let
T
be a
G
-join tree. The
T
-pathset complexity
of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})
, is defined inductively as follows:

•

If
T
is a single node labeled by
G
(in which case
\|G\|\leq 1
), then

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\|G\|=0$ or $\mathcal{A}$ is empty},\\
1&\text{if $\|G\|=1$ and $\mathcal{A}$ is nonempty}.\end{cases}

•

If
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
where
T_{1},T_{2}
are
G_{1},G_{2}
-join trees, then

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vphantom{\big|}\text{sequences }\{(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})\}_{i}\,:\\
\vphantom{|}\smash{(\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{i},\mathcal{C}_{i})\,\in\,\mathscr{P}_{G}\times\mathscr{P}_{G_{1}}\times\mathscr{P}_{G_{2}}},\\
\vphantom{\big|}\mathcal{A}_{i}\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{B}_{i}\,\bowtie\,\mathcal{C}_{i},\ \mathcal{A}\,\subseteq\,\bigcup_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\end{subarray}}\,\sum_{i}\ \max\{\chi_{T_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{i}),\,\chi_{T_{2}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})\}.

Here
i
ranges over an arbitrary finite index set.

The following lower bound on pathset complexity was first proved in
[
23
]
, then again in
[
15
]
with an improved big-
\Omega
constant.

Theorem 6.9

(
[
15
,
23
]
)
.

For every
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree
T
and
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset
\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq\tilde{n}^{\Omega(\log k)}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A}).

6.4
Lower bounds on pathset complexity

The main results of this section (Theorem
6.11
and Corollary
6.12
) give tradeoffs for pathset complexity
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})
with respect to the
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
- and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth of the join tree
T
. These lower bounds are based on the following lemma.

Lemma 6.10
.

For every
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree
T
and
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset
\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq\tilde{n}^{\Psi(T)}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A}).

Proof.

Fix an enumerate
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
of a
T
-branch covering of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
such that
\Psi(T)=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
.
We first observe that pathset complexity
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})
is invariant under
rotations
of
T
, that is, exchanging the left and right children of any subtree. Noting that the theorem statement has nothing to do with
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth (which is obviously not invariant under rotations), we may assume without loss of generality that
\{G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\}
is the
T
-branch covering associated with the right spine of
T
. That is, assume that
T=\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
where each
T_{j}
is a
B_{j}
-join tree and
(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})=(B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(m)})
for some permutation
\smash{\pi:[m]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}[m]}
.

For each
j\in[m]
, let

\displaystyle A_{j}\vcentcolon=B_{j}\cup\dots\cup B_{m},\qquad S_{j}\vcentcolon=\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{j},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle.

Note that
S_{j}
is an
A_{j}
-join tree. In the case
j=1
, we have
S_{1}=T
and
A_{1}=\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.

By definition of pathset complexity, for all
j\in[m-1]
and indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1}
, there exist nonempty pathsets
\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1}}\in\mathscr{P}_{A_{j}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}\in\mathscr{P}_{B_{j}}
such that, letting
\mathcal{A}_{()}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}
, for all
j\in[m-1]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\subseteq\>\bigcup_{i_{j}}\ (\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}),

\displaystyle\chi_{S_{j}}(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{j}}\ \max\{\chi_{T_{j}}(\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}}),\,\chi_{S_{j+1}}(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}})\}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})=\chi_{S_{1}}(\mathcal{A}_{()})\geq\sum_{i_{1}}\chi_{S_{2}}(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}})\geq\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}\chi_{S_{3}}(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},i_{2}})\geq\cdots\geq\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}\chi_{S_{m}}(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{j-1}})=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}1.

Similarly, letting
\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1},1}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{()}\subseteq\>\bigcup_{i_{1}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1}}\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}\>

\displaystyle\subseteq\>\bigcup_{i_{1}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1}}\bowtie\ \bigcup_{i_{2}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\>\subseteq\>\cdots

\displaystyle\cdots\>

\displaystyle\subseteq\>\bigcup_{i_{1}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1}}\bowtie\ \bigcup_{i_{2}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\bowtie\ \bigcup_{i_{3}}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\ \bigcup_{\!\!\!\!i_{m-1}\!\!\!\!}\ \mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}\bowtie\mathcal{A}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}

\displaystyle=\>\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}\\
i_{m}=1\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}_{i_{1}}\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}}\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}}.

We may now bound
\mu(\mathcal{A})
as follows.

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}\\
i_{m}=1\end{subarray}}\,\mu(\mathcal{B}_{i_{1}}\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}\\
i_{m}=1\end{subarray}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\,\mu(\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\pi(j)}}\mid B_{\pi(1)}\cup B_{\pi(2)}\cup\dots\cup B_{\pi(j-1)})\quad\ \text{(by Corollary \ref{cor:density})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}\\
i_{m}=1\end{subarray}}\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\,(1/\tilde{n})^{\Delta(B_{\pi(j)}\,\mid\,B_{\pi(1)}\cup B_{\pi(2)}\cup\dots\cup B_{\pi(j-1)})}\quad\ \text{(since each $\mathcal{B}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{\pi(j)}}$ is a $B_{\pi(j)}$-pathset)}

\displaystyle=(1/\tilde{n})^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(m)})}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{m-1}\\
i_{m}=1\end{subarray}}1\ \ \leq\ \ (1/\tilde{n})^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(m)})}\cdot\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A}).

Since
(B_{\pi(1)},\dots,B_{\pi(m)})=(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
, we get the desired bound

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\ \

\displaystyle\geq\ \ \tilde{n}^{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Delta\hfil$\crcr}}}(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})\ \ =\ \ \tilde{n}^{\Psi(T)}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A}).\qed

As an immediate corollary of Lemma
6.10
and Theorem
3.6
, we get the following tradeoff for pathset complexity.

Theorem 6.11

(Pathset complexity tradeoffs)
.

For every
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree
T
and
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset
\mathcal{A}
,

(I)

\displaystyle\vphantom{\Big|}\smash{\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq\tilde{n}^{\frac{1}{30\mathrm{e}}dk^{1/d}-d}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})}

where
d
is the
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth of
T
,

(II)

\vphantom{\big|}\displaystyle\smash{\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq\tilde{n}^{\frac{1}{\sqrt{32\mathrm{e}}}dk^{1/2d}-d}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})}

where
d
is the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth of
T
.

We remark that Theorem
6.11
does not imply Theorem
6.9
, since the bounds of Theorem
6.11
are trivial for
d=\Omega(k)
while Theorem
6.9
applies to join trees of arbitrary
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
- and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth.
The “maximally overlapping”
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree provides an example which has

\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
- and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
k-1
and
\Psi
-value
1
.
Together these bounds imply:

Corollary 6.12
.

Suppose that
\tilde{n}=n^{\Omega(1)}
(for example,
\tilde{n}=n^{1-\frac{1}{k}}
in our application in this paper). Then for every
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree
T
and
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset
\mathcal{A}
,

(I)

\displaystyle\vphantom{\Big|}\smash{\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})}

where
d
is the
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth of
T
,

(II)

\vphantom{\big|}\displaystyle\smash{\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\geq n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/2d}-1))}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})}

where
d
is the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth of
T
.

The lower bounds of Corollary
6.12
are asymptotically tight for every fixed
d
and
k
, and both bounds converge to
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
since
d(k^{1/d}-1)
converges to
\ln k
as
d\to\infty
.

7
Tradeoffs for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas

In this section we prove
Theorem
1.6
, our size-depth tradeoffs for
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
and
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas in both the monotone and non-monotone settings.
In §
7.1
we discuss two different ways of converting
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
and
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula to DeMorgan formulas with binary
\wedge
and
\vee
gates.
We then prove tradeoff (I)
+
over the course of §
7.2
–
7.5
.
At a high-level, the proof is a reduction that starts out with a monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formula of
\bigwedge
-depth
d
and size
s
computing
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
. The reduction extracts a
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree
T
of
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
d
and a
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[n]^{\{0,\dots,k\}}
of density
n^{-O(1)}
such that
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})\leq n^{O(1)}\cdot s
.
On the other hand, the pathset complexity lower bound of §
6
(Corollary
6.12
) implies
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{A})=n^{\smash{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{A})
.
Combining these inequalities, we get the desired size lower bound
s=n^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})-O(1)}
(
=n^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
since
dk^{1/d}\geq\log k
).

We next prove tradeoff (II)
-
for non-monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas in §
7.6
–
7.8
via a novel reduction that makes a surprising connection between
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula depth to
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of join trees.
The remaining two tradeoffs (I)
-
and (II)
+
of Theorem
1.6
(for non-monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas and monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas) are proved by a “convex combination” of arguments in the proofs of (I)
+
of (II)
-
.
We omit the redundant details.

Proviso 7.1
.

Throughout this section, we assume that
d\leq\log k
and either
k\leq\log\log n
or
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
in the monotone and non-monotone settings, respectively.
In all statements involving pathsets and pathset complexity, the density parameter
\tilde{n}
is fixed to
n^{(k-1)/k}
.

7.1
Converting
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas to DeMorgan formulas

Definition 7.2

(DeMorgan formulas)
.

A
DeMorgan formula
is a rooted binary tree with non-leafs (“gates”) labeled by
\wedge
or
\vee
, and leaves (“inputs”) labeled by constants or literals.
As with
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas, we measure
size
by the number of inputs labeled by literals and
depth
(resp.
\wedge
-depth
) by the maximum number of gates (resp.
\wedge
-gates) on a root-to-leaf branch.
Additionally, we define
left-depth
(resp.
\wedge
-left-depth
) of a DeMorgan formula as the maximum number of left descents (resp. left descents at
\wedge
-gates) on a root-to-leaf branch.

Our tradeoffs for
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
and
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas involve
two different ways of converting an
\mathit{(S)}\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
F
to an equivalent DeMorgan formula
f
.

Definition 7.3

(DeMorgan conversions of
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas)
.

•

The
right-deep DeMorgan conversion
of an
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
F
is the DeMorgan formula
f
obtained by replacing each
m
-ary each fan-in
m

\bigvee
/
\bigwedge
gate with a right-deep tree of
m-1
binary
\vee
/
\wedge
gates.

For example, if
\textup{{F}}=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{8}\textup{{F}}_{i}
, then its right-deep DeMorgan conversion is the formula

\displaystyle\textup{{f}}=\textup{{f}}_{1}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{2}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{3}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{4}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{5}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{6}\wedge(\textup{{f}}_{7}\wedge\textup{{f}}_{8}))))))

where each
\textup{{f}}_{i}
is the right-deep DeMorgan conversion of
\textup{{F}}_{i}
.

•

The
balanced DeMorgan conversion
of an
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
F
is the DeMorgan formula
f
obtained by replacing each
m
-ary each fan-in
m

\bigvee
/
\bigwedge
gate with a balanced tree of
m-1
binary
\vee
/
\wedge
gates.

For example, if
\textup{{F}}=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{8}\textup{{F}}_{i}
, then its balanced DeMorgan conversion is the formula

\displaystyle\textup{{f}}=((\textup{{f}}_{1}\vee\textup{{f}}_{2})\vee(\textup{{f}}_{3}\vee\textup{{f}}_{4}))\vee((\textup{{f}}_{5}\vee\textup{{f}}_{6})\vee(\textup{{f}}_{7}\vee\textup{{f}}_{8}))

where each
\textup{{f}}_{i}
is the balanced DeMorgan conversion of
\textup{{F}}_{i}
.

Note that if
F
has depth
d
and fan-in
m
, then its balanced DeMorgan conversion has depth at most
d\lceil\log m\rceil
. If
F
has
\bigwedge
-fan-in
d
and
\bigwedge
-fan-in
m
, then its right-deep DeMorgan conversion has
\wedge
-left-depth
d
and
\wedge
-depth at most
dm
(though possibly much greater depth). Both conversions preserve size.

Our first tradeoff for
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas (§
7.2
–
7.5
) uses the right-deep DeMorgan conversion to establish a connection between
\bigwedge
-fan-in,
\wedge
-left-depth, and
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth of join trees.
Our second tradeoff for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas (§
7.6
–
7.8
) relies on a randomized version of the balanced DeMorgan conversion to establish a connection between
\mathit{AC^{0}}
depth and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of join trees.
We state the definition here, but postpone analysis to §
7.6
.

Definition 7.4

(
Randomized balanced DeMorgan conversion)
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
be an arbitrary parameter.
For every
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
F
, we define a random DeMorgan formula
f
with distribution
\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}})
as follows:

•

If
F
has depth
0
(i.e., is a constant or literal), then
\textup{{f}}=\textup{{F}}
(with probability
1
).

•

Suppose that
F
is
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\textup{{F}}_{i}
(resp.
\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}\textup{{F}}_{i}
). Sample independent random DeMorgan formulas
\textup{{f}}_{i}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}}_{i})
and independent uniform random indices
\bm{i}_{1},\dots,\bm{i}_{tm}\in[m]
.
Now let
f
be the DeMorgan formula consisting of a balanced tree of
tm-1
binary
\wedge
-gates (resp.
\vee
-gates) with subformulas
\textup{{f}}_{\bm{i}_{1}},\dots,\textup{{f}}_{\bm{i}_{tm}}
feeding in.

Note that if
F
has depth
d
and size
s
, then every
f
in the support of
\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}})
has depth at most
d\lceil\log(ts)\rceil
and size at most
t^{d}s
. In particular, if
t=(\log s)^{O(1)}
and
d=o(\frac{\log s}{\log\log s})
, then
f
has size
s^{1+o(1)}
.
Also note that
f
does not necessarily compute the same function as
F
. However, for
t\geq(\log s)^{2}
, we at least ensure that
\textup{{f}}(x)=\textup{{F}}(x)
with high probability for any given input
x
.

We conclude this subsection with a few remarks on notation.

Notation 7.5
.

Throughout this section, we speak of both
\mathit{AC^{0}}
and DeMorgan formulas on both
kn^{2}
variables and
k
variables. To keep these objects straight, we consistently write

•

F
for an

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula on
kn^{2}
variables,

•

f
for a DeMorgan formula on
kn^{2}
variables,

•

G
for an

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula on
k
variables,

•

g
for a DeMorgan formula on
k
variables.

We use typewriter font
\textup{{F}},\textup{{f}}
and
\textup{{G}},\textup{{g}}
to distinguish these formulas from the Boolean functions of
kn^{2}
and
k
variables that they compute, denoted by italic
f
and
g
.

In the context of tradeoffs (I)
±
, we refer to
F
as an “
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formula” whenever we assume that it has
\bigwedge
-fan-in at most
n^{1/k}
.

Notation 7.6
.

Note the distinction between
syntactic
equality
of formulas (notated with
=
) and
semantic
equivalence
of formulas (notated with
\equiv
).
For example,
\textup{{f}}_{1}=\textup{{f}}_{2}
expresses that
\textup{{f}}_{1}
and
\textup{{f}}_{2}
are isomorphic as labeled binary trees, whereas

\textup{{g}}_{1}\equiv\textup{{g}}_{2}

expresses that

\textup{{g}}_{1}
and
\textup{{g}}_{2}

compute the same Boolean function
\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
.
We sometimes write
\equiv
between an
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula and an equivalent DeMorgan formula (e.g.,
\textup{{F}}\equiv\textup{{f}}
or
\textup{{G}}\equiv\textup{{g}}
).

7.2
Monotone functions on
kn^{2}
variables

In the context of problems
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
,
\textup{{pmm}}_{n,k}
, we identify elements of
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}
with
k
-tuples
\vec{M}=(M^{(1)},\dots,M^{(k)})
of Boolean matrices
M^{(i)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
.
However, for purposes of our lower bound method, it is convenient to instead identify
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}
with the set of subgraphs of the
n
-blow-up of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, defined below.

Definition 7.7

(The
n
-blow-up of a graph)
.

The
n
-blow-up
of a graph
G
, denoted
G^{\uparrow n}
, is the graph with vertex and edge sets

\displaystyle V(G^{\uparrow n})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{v^{(a)}:v\in V(G),\ a\in[n]\}\quad\text{(a way of writing $V(G)\times[n]$)},

\displaystyle E(G^{\uparrow n})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\{v^{(a)},w^{(b)}\}:\{v,w\}\in E(G),\ a,b\in[n]\}.

That is, for each vertex
v
of
G
, the blow-up contains
n
distinct vertices named
v^{(1)},\dots,v^{(n)}
; and for each edge
\{v,w\}
of
G
, the blow-up contains the complete bipartite graph between sets
\{v^{(1)},\dots,v^{(n)}\}
and
\{w^{(1)},\dots,w^{(n)}\}
.

For
\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}
,
we write
G^{(\alpha)}
for the isomorphic copy of
G
with

\displaystyle V(G^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{v^{(\alpha_{v})}:v\in V(G)\},

\displaystyle E(G^{(\alpha)})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\{v^{(\alpha_{v})},w^{(\alpha_{w})}\}:\{v,w\}\in E(G)\}.

Graphs
G^{(\alpha)}

are called
sections
of the blow-up
G^{\uparrow n}
.

To simplify notation: given a section
G^{(\alpha)}
of
G^{\uparrow n}
and a subgraph
H\subseteq G
, we write
H^{(\alpha)}
instead of
H^{(\alpha_{V(H)})}
for the corresponding section of
H^{\uparrow n}
.

Definition 7.8

(Identifying
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}
with the set of subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
)
.

•

The blow-up
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
has
kn^{2}
edges and
2^{kn^{2}}
subgraphs. (Recall that
graphs
have no isolated vertices according to our definition.)

•

For
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and
\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}
, we refer to graphs
G^{(\alpha)}
as
G
-sections
of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
.

•

We identify elements of
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}
with subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
, as well as
k
-tuples of
n
-by-
n
Boolean matrices.

Definition 7.9

(
G
-minterms)
.

For a monotone function
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
, we write
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)
for the
G
-relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}:G^{(\alpha)}\text{ is a minterm of }f\}.

That is,
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)
is the set of
\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}
such that
f(G^{(\alpha)})=1
and
f(H^{(\alpha)})=0
for all
H\subsetneqq G
.

For example, we have
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k})=\{\alpha\in[n]^{\{0,\dots,k\}}:\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{k}=1\}
.
More generally:

Observation 7.10
.

A monotone function
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
computes
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
(i.e., agrees with
\textup{{bmm}}_{n,k}
whenever the input is a
k
-tuple of sub-permutation matrices) if, and only if,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f)=\{\alpha\in[n]^{\{0,\dots,k\}}:\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{k}=1\}
. In this case, we have
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f))=n^{-2}
.

The next lemma relates the
G
-minterm relations of
f_{1}\vee f_{2}
and
f_{1}\wedge f_{2}
to those of
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
.

Lemma 7.11
.

For every graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and monotone functions
f_{1},f_{2}:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
, we have

•

\displaystyle\vphantom{\Big|}\mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{1}\vee f_{2})\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{1})\cup\mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{2})
,

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{1}\wedge f_{2})\ \subseteq\ \!\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\!\!G_{1},G_{2}\,\subseteq\,G\,:\!\!\\
\vphantom{\textstyle|}\!\!\!\!G_{1}\,\cup\,G_{2}\,=\,G\!\!\!\!\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\mathcal{M}_{G_{1}}(f_{1})\bowtie\mathcal{M}_{G_{2}}(f_{2})\ \subseteq\ \mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{1})\cup\mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{2})\cup\!\!\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\!\!G_{1},G_{2}\,\subsetneqq\,G\,:\!\!\\
\vphantom{\textstyle|}\!\!\!\!G_{1}\,\cup\,G_{2}\,=\,G\!\!\!\!\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\mathcal{M}_{G_{1}}(f_{1})\bowtie\mathcal{M}_{G_{2}}(f_{2}).

Proof.

These containments follow from the elementary observation: for any monotone Boolean functions
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, every minterm of
h_{1}\vee h_{2}
is a minterm of
h_{1}
or a minterm of
h_{2}
, and every minterm of
h_{1}\wedge h_{2}
is the union of a minterm of
h_{1}
and a minterm of
h_{2}
.
∎

The following corollary extends Lemma
7.11
to
m
-ary disjunctions and conjunctions. We do not directly use Corollary
7.12
in what follows, but similar reasoning shows up in the proof of Lemma
7.17
.

Corollary 7.12

(
m
-ary version of Lemma
7.11
)
.

For every nonempty graph
\emptyset\neq G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, monotone functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
, we have

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{G}(\bigvee_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})\ \subseteq\ \bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\,\mathcal{M}_{G}(f_{j}),

•

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{G}(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})\ \subseteq\ \!\!\!\!\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vphantom{\textstyle|}\!\!G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\,\subseteq\,G\,:\!\!\\
\vphantom{\textstyle|}\!\!G_{1}\,\cup\,\cdots\,\cup\,G_{m}\,=\,G\!\!\end{subarray}}\ \bigbowtie_{j=1}^{m}\ \mathcal{M}_{G_{j}}(f_{j})\ \subseteq\ \bigcup_{t=1}^{\|G\|}\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\vphantom{\textstyle|}G_{1},\dots,G_{t}\,\subseteq\,G\,:\\
\vphantom{\textstyle|}G_{1}\,\cup\,\cdots\,\cup\,G_{t}\,=\,G,\\
\vphantom{\textstyle|}\hskip-10.0pt\forall s\,\in\,[t],\>G_{s}\,\nsubseteq\,G_{1}\,\cup\,\cdots\,\cup\,G_{s-1}\hskip-10.0pt\end{subarray}}\bigcup_{\vphantom{\textstyle|}1\,\leq\,j_{1}\,<\,\cdots\,<\,j_{t}\,\leq\,m}\ \,\bigbowtie_{s=1}^{t}\ \mathcal{M}_{G_{s}}(f_{j_{s}}).

In the bottom inclusions
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}f_{j})\subseteq\bigcup\bigbowtie\ldots\subseteq\bigcup\bigcup\bigcup\bigbowtie\ldots
, the lefthand union is indexed over
(2^{\|G\|}-1)^{m}
(
\leq 2^{km}
) possibilities for
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
, while the righthand unions are indexed over at most
2^{\smash{\|G\|^{2}}}m^{\|G\|}
(
\leq 2^{\smash{k^{2}}}m^{k}
) possibilities for
t,j_{1},\dots,j_{t},G_{1},\dots,G_{t}
.
In particular, when
k\leq\log\log n
and
m\leq n^{1/k}
, there are only
2^{k^{2}}m^{k}=n^{O(1)}
indices in the righthand unions.
This is essentially the reason why our
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formula lower bounds extend to
\bigwedge
-fan-in
n^{1/k}
.

7.3
Strict join trees

Definition 7.13

(Strict join trees)
.

For
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, a
G
-join tree
T
is
strict
if either
\|G\|\leq 1
and
T
is a single node labeled
G
, or
\|G\|\geq 2
and
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
where
T_{1},T_{2}
are
G_{1},G_{2}
-join trees with
G_{1},G_{2}\subsetneqq G
. We denote the set of strict
G
-join trees by
\mathscr{T}_{G}
.

A simple counting argument shows that
|\mathscr{T}_{G}|\leq 2^{2^{\|G\|}}
. For bounded
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
- or
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth, we get an even better bound:

Lemma 7.14
.

For all
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, there are at most
2^{\|G\|^{d+1}}
distinct
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
with
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth
(
or
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth
)
at most
d
.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
d
. Let
\ell\vcentcolon=\|G\|
and assume
\ell\geq 1
, since otherwise
\mathscr{T}_{G}
is empty. In the case
d=0
, we have
|\mathscr{T}_{G}|\leq 1
. In the case
d=1
, we have
|\mathscr{T}_{G}|=\ell!<2^{\ell^{2}}
.

Suppose
d\geq 2
and consider any
T=\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
where
m\geq 2
and each
T_{j}
is a
G_{j}
-join tree of
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth at most
d-1
. Note that
G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{m}=G
and
G_{j}\nsubseteq G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{j-1}
for all
j\in[m]
.
It follows that
m\leq\ell
, and there are at most
2^{\ell^{2}}
possible sequences
(G_{1},\dots,G_{m})
.

For each
j\in[m]
, since
\|G_{j}\|\leq\ell-1
, there are at most
2^{(\ell-1)^{d}}
possibilities for
T_{j}
by the induction hypothesis.
Therefore, the number of possible
T
is at most

\displaystyle 2^{\ell^{2}}\cdot(2^{(\ell-1)^{d}})^{\ell}=2^{\ell^{2}+\ell(\ell-1)^{d}}\leq 2^{\ell^{d+1}},

since
\ell^{2}+\ell(\ell-1)^{d}\leq\ell^{d+1}
for all
\ell\geq 1
and
d\geq 2
.

The exact same argument applies to
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth, since
\llbracket T_{1},\dots,T_{m}\rrbracket\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
also implies that
G_{j}\nsubseteq G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{j-1}
for all
j\in[m]
.
∎

Definition 7.15
.

Let
f
be a monotone DeMorgan formula on
kn^{2}
variables. For each graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and strict join tree
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
, we define a subset
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(T)}(\textup{{f}})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}})
inductively as follows.
If
\|G\|\leq 1
, then
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\vcentcolon=\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}})
.
If
\|G\|\geq 2
and
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\emptyset\vphantom{\Big|}&\text{if $\textup{{f}}$ has depth $0$},\\
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\cap\Big(\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(T)}(\textup{{f}}_{1})\cup\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(T)}(\textup{{f}}_{2})\Big)&\text{if $\textup{{f}}=\textup{{f}}_{1}\vee\textup{{f}}_{2}$},\\
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\cap\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{1})\cup\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{2})\cup\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(T_{1})}_{G_{1}}(\textup{{f}}_{1})\bowtie\mathcal{M}^{(T_{2})}_{G_{2}}(\textup{{f}}_{2})\Big)\Big)&\text{if $\textup{{f}}=\textup{{f}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{f}}_{2}$}.\end{cases}

The next lemma follows directly from Lemma
7.11
and the definition of subsets
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(T)}(\textup{{f}})
.

Lemma 7.16
.

For every monotone DeMorgan formula
f
on
kn^{2}
variables and graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\bigcup_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}}\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})=\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}).

Moreover, if
f
has
\wedge
-left-depth
d
, then
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})
is nonempty only for
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
with
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth at most
d
.
It follows that there exists a strict join tree
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
with
\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle
-depth at most
d
such that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))\ \geq\ \frac{\mu(\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))}{|\{T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}:T\text{ has $\langle\!\hskip-0.7pt\langle\rangle\!\hskip-0.7pt\rangle$-depth at most $d$}\}|}\ \geq\ \frac{\mu(\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))}{2^{\|G\|^{d+1}}}.

In the event that
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})
is a
G
-pathset for every graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
, we are able to bound on the size of
f
in terms of the pathset complexity of subsets
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})
.

Lemma 7.17

(Lemma 6.6 of
[
23
]
)
.

Suppose that
f
is a monotone DeMorgan formula on
kn^{2}
variables with
\wedge
-depth
D
such that
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})

is a
G
-pathset for every graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
.
Then for every
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))\ \leq\ \binom{D+\|G\|-1}{\|G\|-1}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})\ \leq\ (D+1)^{\|G\|}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}).

Proof.

We prove the inequality by induction on
f
. In the base case where
f
is a constant, or
f
is a positive literal and
\|G\|\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}})
is empty, hence
\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))=0
and
\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})=0
. In the remaining base case that
f
is a positive literal and
\|G\|=1
, we
\chi_{T}(\cdot)\leq 1
and
\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})=1
.

The induction step is straightforward when
f
is a disjunction
\textup{{f}}_{1}\vee\textup{{f}}_{2}
.
We are left with the case that
f
is a conjunction
\textup{{f}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{f}}_{2}
. Here the induction step is trivial when
\|G\|=1
. So we assume that
\|G\|\geq 2
and
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, note that
\|G_{i}\|\leq\|G\|-1
and
0pt_{\wedge}(\textup{{f}}_{i})\leq D-1
and
\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})=\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}_{1})+\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}_{2})
.
By definition of pathset complexity
\chi_{T}(\cdot)
and the induction hypothesis applied to
\textup{{f}}_{1}
and
\textup{{f}}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))

\displaystyle=\chi_{T}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}})\cap\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{1})\cup\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{2})\cup\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(T_{1})}_{G_{1}}(\textup{{f}}_{1})\bowtie\mathcal{M}^{(T_{2})}_{G_{2}}(\textup{{f}}_{2})\Big)\Big)\Big)\vphantom{\bigg|}

\displaystyle\leq\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{1}))+\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{2}))+\max\big\{\chi_{T_{1}}(\mathcal{M}^{(T_{1})}_{G_{1}}(\textup{{f}}_{1})),\>\chi_{T_{2}}(\mathcal{M}^{(T_{2})}_{G_{2}}(\textup{{f}}_{2}))\big\}\vphantom{\bigg|}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Big(\binom{0pt_{\wedge}(\textup{{f}}_{i})+\|G\|-1}{\|G\|-1}+\binom{0pt_{\wedge}(\textup{{f}}_{i})+\|G_{i}\|-1}{\|G_{i}\|-1}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}\Big(\binom{D+\|G\|-2}{\|G\|-1}+\binom{D+\|G\|-2}{\|G\|-2}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}_{i})

\displaystyle=\binom{D+\|G\|-1}{\|G\|-1}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}).\qed\vphantom{{}_{\Big|}}

Corollary 7.18
.

Suppose that
f
is a monotone DeMorgan formula on
kn^{2}
variables with
\wedge
-left-depth
d
and
\wedge
-depth
D
such that
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})
is a
G
-pathset for every graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot(D+1)^{k}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}})).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.16
, there exists a strict join tree
T\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}
such that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}))\geq\frac{1}{2^{k^{d+1}}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}})).

We now obtain the desired bound as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{(D+1)^{k}}\cdot\chi_{T}(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}))

(by Lemma
7.17
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{(D+1)^{k}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}^{(T)}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}))

(by Corollary
6.12
(I))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot(D+1)^{k}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}})).

The hypothesis of Corollary
7.18
requires that
G
-minterm relations
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})
are pathsets for all subformulas
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
. We point out that this condition may fail to hold even for relatively simple DeMorgan formulas
f
.
For example,
suppose
f
is the balanced DeMorgan conversion of the read-once CNF formula
\bigwedge_{i\in[k]}\ \bigvee_{(a,b)\in[n]^{2}}\ M^{(i)}_{a,b}
.
In this case,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}})
is the complete
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-relation
[n]^{\{0,\dots,k\}}
, which is
not
a
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-pathset since it has density
1
(
>\tilde{n}=\tilde{n}^{\Delta(\mathsf{Path}_{k})}
).

7.4
Random restriction lemma

In order to make use of Corollary
7.18
, we require a result (Lemma
7.24
) showing that whenever a monotone function
f:\{0,1\}^{kn}\to\{0,1\}
is computable by a reasonably small
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula of reasonably bounded depth, the
G
-minterm relation of a certain
random restriction
of
f
is a
G
-pathset with very high probability.

Definition 7.19

(Random matrices
\bm{\xi}\leq\bm{\zeta}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
and graphs
{\bm{\Xi}}\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
)
.

•

Let
\bm{\zeta}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be a random Boolean matrix with i.i.d.
\mathrm{Bernoulli}(n^{-1-\frac{1}{2k}})
entries.

•

Let
\bm{\xi}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be the sub-permutation matrix induced by
\bm{\zeta}
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}_{a,b}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\bm{\zeta}_{a,b}=1\text{ and }\bm{\zeta}_{a^{\prime},b}=\bm{\zeta}_{a,b^{\prime}}=0\text{ for all }a^{\prime}\neq a\text{ and }b^{\prime}\neq b,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\qquad

•

Let
\bm{\zeta}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\zeta}^{(k)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be independent random matrices with distribution
\bm{\zeta}
, let
\bm{\xi}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\zeta}^{(k)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be the induced sub-permutation matrices, and let

{\bm{\Xi}}\vcentcolon=(\bm{\xi}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\zeta}^{(k)})
which we regard as an element of
\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}
as well as a subgraph
{\bm{\Xi}}\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
.

Definition 7.20

(The restricted function
f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
)
.

•

For a Boolean function
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
, let
f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
denote the random function defined by
f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}(X)\vcentcolon=f(X\cup{\bm{\Xi}})
for all
X\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{\uparrow n}
. (Note that if
f
is monotone, then so is
f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
.)

•

For a
kn^{2}
-variable DeMorgan formula
f
, let
\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
denote the formula obtained from
f
by substituting the constant
1
(resp.
0
) at each positive (resp. negative) literal corresponding to an edge of
{\bm{\Xi}}
. (Note that if
f
computes
f
, then
\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
computes
f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
.)

The next lemma extends Observation
7.10
concerning the
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-minterms of monotone functions that compute
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
.

Lemma 7.21
.

If
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
is a monotone function which agrees with
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
on every
k
-tuple of sub-permutation matrices, then

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\ \mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\geq\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\ \Big]=1-o(1).

Proof.

Let
\bm{\zeta}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\zeta}^{(k)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
be the random matrices which generate the
\bm{\xi}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{\xi}^{(k)}\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
comprising
{\bm{\Xi}}
.
As noted in Observation
7.10
, we have

\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f)=\{\alpha\in[n]^{\{0,\dots,k\}}:\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{k}=1\}
.
For each
\alpha
in this set, a sufficient condition for
\alpha\in\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))
is that

\bm{\zeta}^{(i)}_{\smash{a,\alpha_{i}}}=\bm{\zeta}^{(i)}_{\smash{\alpha_{i-1},b}}=0

for all
a,b\in[n]
. (In this case,
{\bm{\Xi}}\cup G^{(\alpha)}
is a
k
-tuple of sub-permutation matrices for every
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, from which it follows that
\alpha\in\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))
.)
This sufficient condition occurs with probability
(1-n^{-1-\frac{1}{2k}})^{k(2n-1)}=1-O(kn^{-\frac{1}{2k}})=1-o(1)
.
Therefore, by linearity of expectation,

\displaystyle\Ex\big[\ \mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\ \big]\geq(1-o(1))\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f))=(1-o(1))\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}.

A straightforward application of Janson’s inequality
[
14
]
shows that
\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))
is at least half its expectation with very high probability.
∎

The next definition gives a version of the relation
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)
for non-monotone functions
f
.

Definition 7.22

(The relation
\mathcal{N}_{G}(f)
)
.

•

For a (not necessarily monotone) function
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
and a graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and
\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}
, let
f{\upharpoonright}G^{(\alpha)}:\{0,1\}^{\|G\|}\to\{0,1\}
denote restricted subfunction
f{\upharpoonright}G^{(\alpha)}(H)\vcentcolon=H^{(\alpha)}
where we identify each input
H\in\{0,1\}^{\|G\|}
with the corresponding subgraph of
G
.

•

The relation
\mathcal{N}_{G}(f)\subseteq[n]^{V(G)}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{G}(f)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{\alpha\in[n]^{V(G)}:f{\upharpoonright}G^{(\alpha)}\text{ depends on all $\|G\|$ coordinates}\}.

For monotone functions
f
, note that
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{G}(f)
(hence if
\mathcal{N}_{G}(f)
is a
G
-pathset, then so is
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f)
).

Since it will be useful later, we state here a variant of Lemma
7.17
with
\mathcal{N}_{G}(\cdot)
in place of
\mathcal{M}_{G}(\cdot)
. (The proof is identical.)

Lemma 7.23
.

Suppose that
f
is a DeMorgan formula on
kn^{2}
variables with depth
D
such that
\mathcal{N}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0})

is a
G
-pathset for every graph
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
.
Then for every
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and
T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\chi_{T}(\mathcal{N}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}))

\displaystyle\leq(D+1)^{\|G\|}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}).

We conclude this subsection by stating a key lemma from
[
23
]
, which implies that relations
\mathcal{M}_{G}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
are likely to be
G
-pathsets whenever
f
is computable by monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of reasonable size and depth.

Lemma 7.24

(Lemma 6.5 of
[
23
]
)
.

Suppose that
f:\{0,1\}^{kn^{2}}\to\{0,1\}
is computable by a (not necessarily monotone)
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula of size at most
n^{k}
and depth at most
\frac{\log n}{(\log\log n)^{6}}
. Then for every
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathcal{N}_{G}(f^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\text{ is \lx@text@underline{not} a $G$-pathset}\ \big]

\displaystyle\leq O(n^{-2k}).

7.5
Tradeoff (I)
+
for monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas

We are ready to prove our size-depth tradeoff for monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas.

Theorem 7.25

(Tradeoff (I)
+
of Theorem
1.6
)
.

Suppose that
F
is a monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formula of
\bigwedge
-depth
d
and
\bigwedge
-fan-in
n^{1/k}
which computes
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
where
k\leq\log\log n

and
d\leq\log k
. Then
F
has size
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
F
has size at most
n^{k}
, since this is greater than the lower bound we wish to show. Let
f
be the right-deep DeMorgan conversion of
F
. Note that even though
f
has very large depth,
it has
\wedge
-left-depth
d
and
\wedge
-depth at most
dn^{1/k}
.
Moreover, each subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
is equivalent to a monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula of
\bigwedge
-depth at most
d
(and depth at most
2d+1=O(\log\log n)
) and size at most
n^{k}
. In particular,
\textup{{f}}_{0}
satisfies the hypothesis
of Lemma
7.24
.

We now observe that each of the following statements hold with probability
1-o(1)
:

•

\mathcal{M}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
is a
G
-pathset for every subformula
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
and
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}

(by Lemma
7.24
and a union bound over at most
O(n)^{k}
choices of
\textup{{f}}_{0}
and
G
),

•

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot(dn^{1/k}+1)^{k}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{M}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))

(by Corollary
7.18
),

•

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot(dn^{1/k}+1)^{k}\cdot 2n^{2}}

(by Lemma
7.21
).

Since
\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}})=\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}})\geq\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
,
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot(dn^{1/k}+1)^{k}\cdot 2n^{2}}.

Since
k\leq\log\log n
and
d\leq\log k
, we have
2^{k^{d+1}}\leq n^{o(1)}
and
(dn^{1/k}+1)^{k}=n^{1+o(1)}
, so the denominator above is
n^{O(1)}
, whereas the numerator
is at least
n^{\Omega(\log k)}
.
We conclude that
F
has size
n^{\Omega(d(k^{1/d}-1))}
as required.
∎

Our proof of tradeoff (I)
+
shows that we can slightly extend the range of
k
from
\log\log n
to
2^{\sqrt{\log\log n}}
and
\bigwedge
-fan-in from
n^{1/k}
to
n^{(\log k)/k}
.
While the range of
k
can probably be pushed further, the proof breaks down for larger
\bigwedge
-fan-in
D=n^{\omega((\log k)/k)}
; this is ultimately due to the
D^{k}
factor in the bound of Lemma
7.17
.
Also notice that our
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/d})}
lower bound for monotone
\mathit{SAC^{0}}
formulas of
\bigwedge
-depth
d
cannot possibly extend to unbounded fan-in
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of
\bigwedge
-depth
d
, in light of the
n^{O(dk^{1/(2d+1)})}
upper bound given by the monotone
\Sigma_{2d+1}
formulas of Proposition
1.3
(II).

7.6
Strict support tree of a
k
-variable DeMorgan formula

We next define notions of
strictness
and
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth
for DeMorgan formulas, which are analogous to the corresponding notions for join trees. Even though we only apply these notions to
k
-variable DeMorgan formulas, we state the definitions more generally without reference to
k
.

Definition 7.26

(Strict DeMorgan formulas and operations
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})
and
\mathit{strict}(T)
)
.

•

A DeMorgan formula
g
is
strict
if either

–

it has depth
0
(i.e.,
g
is a constant or literal), or

–

it is
\textup{{g}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{g}}_{2}
or
\textup{{g}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{g}}_{2}
where
\textup{{g}}_{1},\textup{{g}}_{2}
are strict and
compute distinct functions from
g
(i.e.,
\textup{{g}}_{1}\not\equiv\textup{{g}}
and
\textup{{g}}_{2}\not\equiv\textup{{g}}
).

•

For every DeMorgan formula
g
, we define an equivalent strict DeMorgan formula
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})
inductively as follows:

–

if
g
has depth
0
, then
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=\textup{{g}}
,

–

if
g
is
\textup{{g}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{g}}_{2}
(resp.
\textup{{g}}_{1}\vee\textup{{g}}_{2}
), then

\displaystyle\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{1})&\text{if }\textup{{g}}_{1}\equiv\textup{{g}},\\
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{2})&\text{if }\textup{{g}}_{1}\not\equiv\textup{{g}}\text{ and }\textup{{g}}_{2}\equiv\textup{{g}},\\
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{1})\wedge\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{2})\text{ (resp.\ $\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{1})\vee\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{2})$)}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(Note that
g
is strict if, and only if,
\textup{{g}}=\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})
. On the other hand,
\textup{{g}}\equiv\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})
for all
g
.)

•

For every

G
-join tree
T
, we define
a strict
G
-join tree

\mathit{strict}(T)

in a similar fashion:

–

if
T
has
\langle\hskip 0.59998pt\rangle
-depth
0
, then
\mathit{strict}(T)\vcentcolon=T
,

–

if
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
where
T_{1},T_{2}
are
G_{1},G_{2}
-join trees, then

\displaystyle\mathit{strict}(T)\vcentcolon=\begin{cases}\mathit{strict}(T_{1})&\text{if }G_{1}=G,\\
\mathit{strict}(T_{2})&\text{if }G_{1}\neq G\text{ and }G_{2}=G,\\
\langle\mathit{strict}(T_{1}),\mathit{strict}(T_{2})\rangle&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

To help the reader keep track of the types of objects, we use typewriter font for the
strict
operator on DeMorgan formulas and italics for the
\mathit{strict}
operator on join trees. (Later on in Definition
7.29
, we use sans-serif
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
for a subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and italic
S(\textup{{g}})
for a join tree associated with
g
.)

Definition 7.27

(
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket
-depth of DeMorgan formulas)
.

•

For any DeMorgan formulas
\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}
, we define DeMorgan formulas
\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\wedge}
and
\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\vee}
inductively by

\displaystyle\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1}\rrbracket_{\wedge}=\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1}\rrbracket_{\vee}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\textup{{g}}_{1},

\displaystyle\text{for }m=1,\vphantom{\big|}

\displaystyle\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\wedge}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m-1}\rrbracket_{\wedge}\wedge\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m-2},\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\wedge}

\displaystyle\text{for }m\geq 2,\vphantom{\big|}

\displaystyle\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\vee}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m-1}\rrbracket_{\vee}\vee\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m-2},\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\vee}

\displaystyle\text{for }m\geq 2.\vphantom{\big|}

•

The
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth
of a DeMorgan formula
g
is defined inductively as follows:

–

if
g
has depth
0
, then
0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=0
,

–

if
g
has depth
\geq 1
, then

\displaystyle 0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=1+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\,\geq\,2,\ \textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\,:\\
\textup{{g}}\,=\,\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\wedge}\text{ or }\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\vee}\end{subarray}}\max_{i\in[m]}\ 0pt_{\llbracket\hskip 0.49164pt\rrbracket{}}(\textup{{g}}_{i}).

We now restrict attention to
k
-variable DeMorgan formulas, whose variables we identify with edges of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
and whose inputs in
\{0,1\}^{k}
we identify with subgraphs of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.

Lemma 7.28
.

There are at most

2^{2^{(d+1)(k+1)}}

distinct strict
k
-variable DeMorgan formulas of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
.

Proof.

Consider any strict
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth
d
. Without loss of generality,

\displaystyle\textup{{g}}=\llbracket\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}\rrbracket_{\wedge}

for some
\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d-1
. Note that
\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}
are necessarily also strict.

Let
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}:\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
be the functions computes by
\textup{{g}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{m}
. Strictness of
g
implies that

\displaystyle g_{1},\quad g_{1}\wedge g_{2},\quad g_{1}\wedge g_{2}\wedge g_{3},\quad\dots,\quad g_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge g_{m}

is a strictly decreasing sequence of Boolean functions. Moreover,
g_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge g_{m}\not\equiv 0
assuming
m\geq 1
.
It follows that
m\leq 2^{k}
. (Whereas there are
2^{2^{k}}
distinct Boolean functions
\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
, the longest decreasing sequence of non-zero functions has length
2^{k}
.)

We conclude that

\big|\big\{\textup{strict $k$-variable DeMorgan formulas of $\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}$-depth at most $d$}\big\}\big|\\
\leq 2+2k+2\big|\big\{\textup{strict $k$-variable DeMorgan formulas of $\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}$-depth at most $d-1$}\big\}\big|\vphantom{\big|}^{2^{k}}.

(Here the additive
2+2k
counts the depth-
0
formulas, and the other factor
2
arises from the choice of top gate type.) The bound of the lemma follows since
2+2k+2^{2^{d(k+1)}}\leq 2^{2^{(d+1)(k+1)}}
for all
d,k\geq 0
.
∎

The next definition associates a
join tree
S(\textup{{g}})
with each
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
.

Definition 7.29

(Support, restriction, and support tree)
.

•

The
support
of a Boolean function
g:\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
is the set
\mathsf{Supp}(g)\subseteq[k]
of coordinates on which
g
depends, that is,

\displaystyle\mathsf{Supp}(g)

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\{i\in[k]:\exists y\in\{0,1\}^{k},\ g(y)\neq g(y_{1},\dots,y_{i-1},1-y_{i},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{k})\}.

(We identify
\mathsf{Supp}(g)
with the corresponding subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
.)

The
support
of a
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
, denoted
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
, is the support of the Boolean function it computes.

•

For a
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
and a subgraph
H\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
, let
\textup{{g}}{\upharpoonright}H
denote the DeMorgan formula obtained from
g
by syntactically relabeling to
0
(resp.
1
) each leaf labeled by a positive (resp. negative) literal whose coordinate lies in
E(\mathsf{Path}_{k})\setminus E(H)
.

(Observe that
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}}{\upharpoonright}H)\subseteq H
and
\textup{{g}}\equiv\textup{{g}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
, even though
g
and
\textup{{g}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
are not necessarily equal.)

•

For every
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
, we define a join tree
S(\textup{{g}})
, called the
support tree
of
g
, inductively as follows:

–

if
g
is a constant (i.e.,
0
or
1
), then
S(\textup{{g}})
is the empty join tree;

–

if
g
is the
i
th positive or negative literal, then
S(\textup{{g}})
is the single-node join tree labeled by
E_{i}
(i.e., the
i
th single-edge subgraph of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
);

–

if
g
is
\textup{{g}}_{1}\wedge\textup{{g}}_{2}
or
\textup{{g}}_{1}\vee\textup{{g}}_{2}
, then

S(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=S(\textup{{g}}_{1}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}}))\cup S(\textup{{g}}_{2}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})).

Note that
S(\textup{{g}})
is a
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
-join tree. (Had we instead defined
S(\textup{{g}})\vcentcolon=S(\textup{{g}}_{1})\cup S(\textup{{g}}_{2})
, then
S(\textup{{g}})
would be a
G
-join tree where
G
is the supergraph of
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
that contain the edges of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
corresponding to all literals that appear in
g
.)

•

We refer to
\mathit{strict}(S(\textup{{g}}))
as the
strict support tree of
g
. Note that
\mathit{strict}(S(\textup{{g}}))
is a strict
\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})
-join tree (i.e., an element of
\mathscr{T}_{\mathsf{Supp}(\textup{{g}})}
).

The next lemma follows directly from definitions.

Lemma 7.30
.

For every
k
-variable DeMorgan formula
g
, the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of the join tree
S(\textup{{g}})
(and hence also
\mathit{strict}(S(\textup{{g}}))
