) is at most the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of
g
.∎

7.7
The distribution of
\textup{{strict}}(\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}}))
for a
k
-variable
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
G

In this subsection, we prove a key result (Lemma
7.33
) on the distribution of
\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})
for the randomized balanced DeMorgan conversion
\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})
(Def.
7.4
)
of a
k
-variable
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
G
.
We begin with a preliminary lemma on the distribution of
\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{t})
for a certain family of random join trees
\bm{T}_{t}
.

Lemma 7.31
.

Fix any real numbers
p_{1}\geq\dots\geq p_{k}>0
with
p_{1}+\dots+p_{k}=1
.
For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\bm{T}_{t}
be a random join tree of
\langle\hskip 0.59998pt\rangle
-depth
t
with
2^{t}
leaves independently labeled by single-edge graphs
E_{1},\dots,E_{k}
with probability
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
respectively.
Then for all
t\geq\log(\frac{1}{p_{k}})+k
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{t})=\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]\geq\exp(-O(2^{k})).

A key point in Lemma
7.31
is that the bound
\exp(-O(2^{k}))
is independent of
t
and the distribution
p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
).

Proof.

First, consider the case of the uniform distribution

p_{1}=\dots=p_{k}=\frac{1}{k}
.
Here we have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{k})=\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]=\Pr\big[\ \bm{T}_{k}=\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]=k^{-k}.

Note that for any
t\geq k
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{t})=\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]\geq\Pr\big[\ \text{the leftmost depth-$k$ sub-join tree of $\bm{T}_{t}$ equals }\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]=k^{-k}.

To illustrate the general argument,
consider the following (essentially geometric) distribution with
k=4
:

\displaystyle p_{1}\approx 0.99,\qquad p_{2}=2^{-10},\qquad p_{3}=2^{-100},\qquad p_{4}=2^{-1000}.

First, note that

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \bm{T}_{1000}\text{ contains a single }E_{4}\ \big]=\Pr\big[\ \mathrm{Binomial}(2^{1000},2^{-1000})=1\ \big]\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}}.

Letting
\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{L})}
and
\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{R})}
be the left and right children of the root of
\bm{T}_{1000}
, we next observe that

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \underbrace{\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{L})}\text{ contains no }E_{4}\text{ and }\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{R})}\text{ contains a single }E_{4}}_{\textstyle\textup{call this event }\mathcal{E}}\ \big]\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}.

Conditioned on event
\mathcal{E}
, let
\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})}
be the leftmost depth-
k
sub-join tree of
\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{L})}
, and let
\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})}
be the unique depth-
k
sub-join tree of
\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{R})}
that contains
E_{4}
.
Now observe that a sufficient condition for
\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{1000})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4}\rrbracket
is that

(a)

event
\mathcal{E}
holds,

(b)

\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\rrbracket
, and

(c)

\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{4}\rrbracket
.

(Given
\mathcal{E}
, condition (b) implies that
\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\rrbracket
and (c) implies that
\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{999}^{(\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{4}\rrbracket
. Conditions (a),(b),(c) therefore together imply that
\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{1000})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4}\rrbracket
.)
We now have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{1000})={}

\displaystyle\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \big]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\Big[\ \mathcal{E}\text{ and }\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\rrbracket\text{ and }\mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \Big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big].

Splitting
\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})}
(resp.
\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})}
) into left and right subtrees
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{L})}
and
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{R})}
(resp.
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{L})}
and
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{R})}
), we continue this analysis:

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\ \underbrace{\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{L})}\text{ contains no }E_{3}\text{ and }\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{R})}\text{ contains a single }E_{3}}_{\textstyle\textup{call this event }\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{L})}}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}},

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\ \underbrace{\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{L})}\text{ contains no }E_{4}\text{ and }\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{R})}\text{ contains a single }E_{4}}_{\textstyle\textup{call this event }\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{R})}}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}.

Next, for appropriate definitions of sub-join trees
\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{L})},\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{R})}
of
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{L})}
and
\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{L})},\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{R})}
of
\bm{T}_{99}^{(\mathrm{R})}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{L})})={}

\displaystyle\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\wedge\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{L})}\ \Big]\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{L}\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{3}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\wedge\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{L})}\ \Big],

\displaystyle\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{100}^{(\mathrm{R})})={}

\displaystyle\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{L})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\wedge\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{R})}\ \Big]\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{10}^{(\mathrm{R}\mathrm{R})})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\wedge\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{R})}\ \Big].

This analysis continues down one more layer. In the end we get a bound

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{1000})=\llbracket E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4}\rrbracket\ \big]

\displaystyle\gtrsim\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\right)\right)\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{2}}\right)\right)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}{}^{14}}.

Generalizing this example to larger
k
, we get a lower bound

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \mathit{strict}(\bm{T}_{10^{k}})=\llbracket E_{1},\dots,E_{k}\rrbracket\ \big]

\displaystyle\gtrsim\exp(-2(2^{k}-1)),

which is essentially the worst case for the lemma.

To generalize this argument for an arbitrary distribution
p_{1}\geq\dots\geq p_{k}>0
, in place of depths
1,10,100,1000
in the example above, we consider the sequence
1=t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots<t_{k}
defined by

\displaystyle t_{i}\vcentcolon=\Big\lfloor\log\frac{1}{p_{i}+\dots+p_{k}}\Big\rfloor+i.

The same analysis yield the desired
\exp(-O(2^{k}))
lower bound.
∎

Simply rephrasing Lemma
7.31
in terms of depth-
1

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas, we get the following:

Corollary 7.32
.

For every
k
-variable
\Pi_{1}
or
\Sigma_{1}
formula
G
(i.e., an
m
-ary
\bigwedge
or
\bigvee
of literals, where
m
may be arbitrarily large relative to
k
),
there exists an equivalent strict DeMorgan formula
\textup{{G}}^{\ast}
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
1
such that for all
t\geq 2^{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})}\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\ \big]\geq\exp(-O(2^{k})).

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it suffices to consider the case that
G
is a monotone
\Pi_{1}
formula of size
m=m_{1}+\dots+m_{k}
with
m_{i}
leaves labeled by the positive literal
X_{i}
, where
m_{1}\geq\dots\geq m_{k}\geq 1
. Further assume w.l.o.g. that
m
is a power of
2
. Letting
p_{i}\vcentcolon=\frac{m_{i}}{m}
for
i\in[k]
, note that
\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})
is identical (after renaming
\wedge
to
\cup
and
X_{i}
to
E_{i}
) to the random join tree
\bm{T}_{\log(tm)}
of Lemma
7.31
.
Since
\log(tm)\geq k+\log m\geq k+\log\frac{1}{p_{k}}
,
Lemma
7.31
implies

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})}\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\llbracket X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\rrbracket_{\wedge}\ \big]

\displaystyle\geq\exp(-O(2^{k})).

The desired statement is proved by letting
\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\vcentcolon=\llbracket X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\rrbracket_{\wedge}
, which is clearly strict, equivalent to
G
, and has
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth
1
.
∎

The next lemma extends Corollary
7.32
to
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas
G
beyond depth
1
.
For the analysis to work out, we consider the distribution
\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})
for larger
t
, which allows us to condition on the event that
\textup{{g}}_{0}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}}_{0})
is equivalent to
\textup{{G}}_{0}
for every sub-formula
\textup{{G}}_{0}
of
G
.

Lemma 7.33
.

For every
k
-variable
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
G
of depth
d
, there exists an equivalent strict DeMorgan formula
\textup{{G}}^{\ast}
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
such that for all
t\geq 2^{2^{2^{k}}}\cdot(\log\mathrm{size}(\textup{{G}}))^{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})}\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\ \big]\geq 2^{-\exp(O(d2^{2^{k}}))}.

Proof.

The case
d=0
is trivial and Corollary
7.32
proves the case
d=1
.

For the induction step when
d\geq 2
, assume that
\textup{{G}}=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\textup{{G}}_{i}
.
(The argument for
\textup{{G}}=\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}\textup{{G}}_{i}
is identical after swapping
\vee
for
\wedge
.)
By the induction hypothesis, there exist
k
-variable DeMorgan formulas
\textup{{G}}_{1}^{\ast},\dots,\textup{{G}}_{m}^{\ast}
which satisfy the lemma with respect to subformulas
\textup{{G}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{G}}_{m}
.

Let
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}:\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
enumerate the distinct functions computed by sub-formulas
\textup{{G}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{G}}_{m}
,
ordered such that
m_{1}\geq\dots\geq m_{s}
where

\displaystyle m_{j}\vcentcolon=\big|\big\{i\in[m]:\textup{{G}}_{i}\text{ computes }h_{j}\big\}\big|.

Next, for each
j\in[s]
, consider the set

\displaystyle\big\{\textup{{G}}_{i}^{\ast}:i\in[m]\text{ such that $\textup{{G}}_{i}$ computes }h_{j}\big\}.

Each element of this set is a strict
k
-variable DeMorgan formula of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d-1
, hence the size of this set is at most
2^{2^{d(k+1)}}
by Lemma
7.28
.
It follows that there exist strict
k
-variable DeMorgan formulas
\textup{{h}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{s}
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d-1
such that for all
j\in[s]
,

\displaystyle\big|\big\{i\in[m]:\textup{{G}}_{i}^{\ast}=\textup{{h}}_{j}\big\}\big|\geq\frac{m_{j}}{2^{2^{d(k+1)}}}.

Let
J=\{j_{1}<\dots<j_{r}\}\subseteq[s]
be the subset of indices defined by

\displaystyle J\vcentcolon=\big\{j\in[s]:h_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge h_{j-1}\not\equiv h_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge h_{j}\big\}.

Note that whereas
s\leq 2^{2^{k}}
, we have
r\leq 2^{k}
since this is the maximum length of a strictly decreasing sequence of nonzero functions
\{0,1\}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
(assuming w.l.o.g. that
\textup{{G}}\not\equiv 0
).

At last, we choose
\textup{{G}}^{\ast}
to be the formula
\llbracket\textup{{h}}_{j_{1}},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{j_{r}}\rrbracket_{\wedge}
. Observe that
\textup{{G}}^{\ast}
is equivalent to
G
and has
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
. Moreover, it is strict and satisfies

\textup{{strict}}(\llbracket\textup{{h}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{s}\rrbracket_{\wedge})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}
.

Onto the probabilistic analysis.
We generate
\textup{{g}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}})
a balanced tree of binary
\wedge
-gates with subformulas
\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{1}},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{tm}}
feeding in for independent uniform random
\bm{\iota}_{1},\dots,\bm{\iota}_{tm}\in[m]
and
\textup{{g}}_{i}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}}_{i})
(
i\in[m]
). Note that our choice of
t

ensures that
\textup{{g}}_{i}\equiv\textup{{G}}_{i}
for all
i\in[m]
with probability
1-o(1)
. Let us henceforth automatically condition on this event in all instances of
\Pr[\,\cdot\,]
below.

By the induction hypothesis and our choice of formulas
\textup{{h}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{s}
, for all indices
j\in[s]
and
q\in[tm]
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q}})=\textup{{h}}_{j}\ \big|\ \textup{{G}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q}}\textup{ computes }h_{j}\ \big]

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-2^{d(k+1)}}\cdot\Pr\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q}})=G_{\bm{\iota}_{q}}^{\ast}\ \big]

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-2^{d(k+1)}}\cdot 2^{-\exp(O((d-1)2^{2^{k}}))}.

Now consider the top
\log(tm)
layers of
g
, which we view as a random tree of binary
\wedge
gates with inputs labeled by the functions computed at subformulas
\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{1}},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{tm}}
(elements of the set
\{h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\}
). Call this tree
T(\textup{{g}})
.
Viewing
T(\textup{{g}})
as a join tree over single-edge graphs
E_{1},\dots,E_{s}
(or, alternatively, viewing
T(\textup{{g}})
as a DeMorgan formula over literals corresponding to functions
h_{1},\dots,h_{s}
), Lemma
7.31
(or from a different perspective Corollary
7.32
) tells us

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\ \underbrace{\mathit{strict}(T(\textup{{g}}))=\llbracket h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\rrbracket_{\wedge}}_{\textstyle\textup{call this event }\mathcal{E}}\ \big]\geq 2^{-c2^{s}}

for some absolute constant
c
determined by Lemma
7.31
.

Conditioned on event
\mathcal{E}
, there exist
2^{s}
indices — name them
1\leq q_{1}<\dots<q_{2^{s}}<tm
— which embed the leaves of
\llbracket h_{1},\dots,h_{s}\rrbracket_{\wedge}
among the leaves of
T(\textup{{g}})
. By definition of this sequence, formulas

\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{1}}},\dots,\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}}}}
compute functions
h_{1},h_{2},h_{1},h_{3},\dots,h_{1},h_{2},h_{1},h_{s}
.
Now observe that

\displaystyle\Pr\big[\

\displaystyle\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\ \big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \big]

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{strict}}(\llbracket\textup{{h}}_{1},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{s}\rrbracket_{\wedge})\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\geq\Pr\Big[\ (\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{1}}}),\dots,\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}}}}))=(\textup{{h}}_{1},\textup{{h}}_{2},\textup{{h}}_{1},\textup{{h}}_{3},\dots,\textup{{h}}_{1},\textup{{h}}_{2},\textup{{h}}_{1},\textup{{h}}_{s})\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \Big]\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{1}}})=\textup{{h}}_{1}\ \Big|\ \textup{{G}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{1}}}\textup{ computes }h_{1}\ \Big]\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2}}})=\textup{{h}}_{2}\ \Big|\ \textup{{G}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2}}}\textup{ computes }h_{2}\ \Big]\cdot\,\ldots\mbox{}

\displaystyle\quad\ \mbox{}\ldots\,\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}-1}}})=\textup{{h}}_{1}\ \Big|\ \textup{{G}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}-1}}}\textup{ computes }h_{1}\ \Big]\cdot\Pr\Big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}}}})=\textup{{h}}_{s}\ \Big|\ \textup{{G}}_{\bm{\iota}_{q_{2^{s}}}}\textup{ computes }h_{s}\ \Big]

\displaystyle\geq\Big(2^{-2^{d(k+1)}}\cdot 2^{-\exp(O((d-1)2^{2^{k}}))}\Big){}^{2^{s}}.

Finally, we obtain the desired bound

\displaystyle\vphantom{\Big|}\Pr\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\ \big]\geq\Pr\big[\ \mathcal{E}\ \big]\cdot\Pr\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}})=\textup{{G}}^{\ast}\ \big|\ \mathcal{E}\ \big]

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-c2^{s}}\cdot\Big(2^{-2^{d(k+1)}}\cdot 2^{-\exp(O((d-1)2^{2^{k}}))}\Big){}^{2^{s}}

\displaystyle\geq\Big(2^{-c}\cdot 2^{-2^{d(k+1)}}\cdot 2^{-\exp(O((d-1)2^{2^{k}}))}\Big)\smash{{}^{2^{2^{2^{k}}}}}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-\exp(O(d2^{2^{k}}))}

for an appropriate big-
O
constant depending on the constant
c
.
∎

7.8
Tradeoff (II)
-
for non-monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas

We are ready to prove size-depth tradeoff (II)
-
for non-monotone
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas. The proof has an overall similar structure to the proof of tradeoff (I)
+
in §
7.5
.

Theorem 7.34

(Tradeoff (II)
-
of Theorem
1.6
)
.

Suppose that
F
is a depth-
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula
which computes
\textup{{sub-pmm}}_{n,k}
where
k\leq\log^{\ast}n

and
d\leq\log k
. Then
F
has size
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}
.

By a simple additional argument, the same
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}
lower bound may actually be obtained for
\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas of depth
d+1
(precisely matching the statement of tradeoff (II)
-
in Theorem
1.6
). We present the lower bound for depth-
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formulas for simplicity sake, since the factor
2
in the second exponent is anyway probably not optimal.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
F
has size at most
n^{k}
,

since this is greater than the lower bound we wish to show.
Let

\displaystyle t\vcentcolon=2^{2^{2^{k}}}\cdot(\log n)^{4}

and consider the randomized balanced DeMorgan conversion
\textup{{f}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}})
.
Lemma
7.24
implies that, with probability
1-o(1)
, the
G
-relations
\mathcal{N}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
are pathsets for all subformulas
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
and subgraphs
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
. We proceed with our analysis conditioned on this almost sure event.

For each
\textup{{f}}_{0}
and
G
, we define a covering
\bigcup_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{G}}\mathcal{N}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})=\mathcal{N}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
by essentially Definition
7.15
, except that in the induction step where
T=\langle T_{1},T_{2}\rangle
, we are now forced to treat the case
\textup{{f}}_{0}=\textup{{f}}_{1}\vee\textup{{f}}_{2}
the same as
\textup{{f}}_{0}=f_{1}\wedge\textup{{f}}_{2}
, in both cases defining

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{G}^{(T)}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\vcentcolon=\mathcal{N}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\cap\Big(\mathcal{N}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{1}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\cup\mathcal{N}^{(T)}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{2}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\cup\Big(\mathcal{N}^{(T_{1})}_{G_{1}}(\textup{{f}}_{1}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\bowtie\mathcal{N}^{(T_{2})}_{G_{2}}(\textup{{f}}_{2}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})\Big)\Big).

By Lemma
7.21
,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})

almost surely has density at least
\frac{1}{2n^{2}}
.
For each
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
, we consider the restricted
k
-variable depth-
d

\mathit{AC^{0}}
formula

\displaystyle\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}

obtained by
F
by fixing variables corresponding to
{\bm{\Xi}}
to
1
, and all other variables except the
k
edges of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}
to
0
.

We now invoke the key Lemma
7.33
to obtain, for each
\alpha\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
, a strict DeMorgan formula
\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast}
of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
, which is equivalent to
\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}
(hence depends on all
k
variables) and satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr_{\textup{{g}}_{\alpha}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}}_{\alpha})}\big[\ \textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\alpha})=\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\ \big]\geq 2^{-\exp(O(d2^{2^{k}}))}.

Let
T_{\alpha}^{\ast}

denote the strict support tree of
\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast}
:

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}^{\ast}\vcentcolon=\mathit{strict}(S(\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast})).

Note that
T_{\alpha}^{\ast}
is a strict
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join tree since
\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}
(
=\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}
) depends on all variables corresponding to edges of
\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}
. Moreover,
T_{\alpha}^{\ast}
has
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
(i.e., at most the
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth of
\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast}
) by Lemma
7.30
.

Next, we observe that the distribution of
\textup{{g}}_{\alpha}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{G}}_{\alpha})
— that is,
\textup{{g}}_{\alpha}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)})
— is
identical
to distribution of
\textup{{f}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}
where
\textup{{f}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
.
Generating
\textup{{g}}_{\alpha}
as
\textup{{f}}{\upharpoonright}\mathsf{Path}_{k}^{(\alpha)}
, we have the following implication:

\displaystyle\textup{{strict}}(\textup{{g}}_{\alpha})=\textup{{G}}_{\alpha}^{\ast}\ \Longrightarrow\ \alpha\in\mathcal{N}^{(T_{\alpha}^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}).

Since there are at most
2^{k^{d+1}}
strict
\mathsf{Path}_{k}
-join trees of
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
(by Lemma
7.14
), we may fix one such join tree
T^{\ast}
such that

\displaystyle\mu(\{\alpha\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}):T_{\alpha}^{\ast}=T^{\ast}\})\geq\frac{1}{2^{k^{d+1}}}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{N}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\geq\frac{1}{2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot 2n^{2}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mu(\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\geq\frac{1}{2^{\exp(O(d2^{2^{k}}))}\cdot 2^{k^{d+1}}\cdot 2n^{2}}=\frac{1}{n^{2+o(1)}}.

Recall that the random DeMorgan formula
\textup{{f}}\sim\mathscr{D}_{t}(\textup{{F}})
has depth at most
d\cdot\lceil\log(t\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}}))\rceil
and size at most
t^{d}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}})
. Moreover, with probability
1-o(1)
, functions
\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
and
\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}
agree on all inputs of Hamming weight
\leq k
(by a union bound), hence

\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})=\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}).

Since
\mathcal{N}_{G}(\textup{{f}}_{0}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})
are pathsets for all
\textup{{f}}_{0}
of
f
and
G\subseteq\mathsf{Path}_{k}
,
Lemma
7.23

yields an upper bound on pathset complexity:

\displaystyle\chi_{T^{\ast}}(\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\vphantom{\Big|}

\displaystyle=\chi_{T^{\ast}}(\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))

\displaystyle\leq\big(0pt(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})+1\big){}^{k}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{f}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}})

\displaystyle=O\big(d\cdot\log(t\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}}))\big){}^{k}\cdot t^{d}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}})

\displaystyle\leq O\big(d\cdot\log(2^{2^{2^{k}}}\cdot(\log n)^{4}\cdot n^{k})\big){}^{k}\cdot 2^{d2^{2^{k}}}\cdot(\log n)^{4d}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}})

\displaystyle=n^{o(1)}\cdot\mathrm{size}(\textup{{F}}).

On the other hand, since
T^{\ast}
has
\llbracket\hskip 0.59998pt\rrbracket{}
-depth at most
d
, Corollary
6.12
(II) provides the lower bound

\displaystyle\chi_{T^{\ast}}(\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))

\displaystyle\geq n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}\cdot\mu(\mathcal{N}^{(T^{\ast})}_{\mathsf{Path}_{k}}(\textup{{F}}^{\cup{\bm{\Xi}}}))\geq\frac{n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}}{n^{2+o(1)}}.

Combining these inequalities, we conclude that
F
has size
n^{\Omega(dk^{1/2d})}
as required.
∎

Remark 7.35
.

This proof shows that the

range
k\leq\log^{\ast}n
can be extended to
k\leq 0.99\log\log\log\log n
, small enough to ensure that
2^{\exp(O(d2^{\smash{2^{k}}}))}=n^{o(1)}
. We have made little effort to optimize the range of
k
and expect it can be extended further, potentially to
\log\log n
.
